On completeness and parametricity in the realizability semantics of System 𝖥 \mathsf{F}
Paolo Pistone

Abstract
. We investigate completeness and parametricity for a general class of realizability semantics for System 𝖥 \mathsf{F} defined in terms of closure operators over sets of λ \lambda -terms. This class includes most semantics used for normalization theorems, as those arising from Tait’s saturated sets and Girard’s reducibility candidates. We establish a completeness result for positive types which subsumes those existing in the literature, and we show that closed realizers satisfy parametricity conditions expressed either as invariance with respect to logical relations or as dinaturality. Our results imply that, for positive types, typability, realizability and parametricity are equivalent properties of closed normal λ \lambda -terms.



Feb. 17, 2018Oct. 29, 2019

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 15, Issue 4, Paper 6

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we call realizability semantics for typed

\lambda
-calculi interpretations of types with well-behaved sets of
\lambda
-terms (or, more generally, with subsets of some
partial combinatory algebra

[
Str91
]
),
called the realizers of the type.
System
\mathsf{F}
came equipped since its birth in
[
Gir72
]
with two realizability semantics. First, the

reducibility candidates
semantics, which constituted the starting point for the now common use of realizability to prove normalization theorems for higher order type theories (see
[
Gal90
,
Kri93
]
). Second, the semantics of
partial equivalence relations (
\mathsf{PER}
)
, which constituted the starting point for the mathematical investigation of realizability models, leading to striking categorical structures (e.g. the
effective topos

[
Hyl88
]
).

Normalization results for typed
\lambda
-calculi are usually proved by establishing the
soundness
of a given realizability semantics,
that is, by showing that all terms of a given type are realizers of that type.
The converse property,
completeness
, that is the fact that all realizers of a type provably have that type, has also been established for some realizability semantics
[
Hin83a
,
HRR89
,
FN98
]
.
While soundness is usually established for full System F,
completeness results are generally restricted to types in which the second order quantifier
\forall
only occurs in positive position (e.g. algebraic types). Following
[
Kri93
]
, we will refer to these types as
positive types
.

A common feature of realizability models is that universal quantification is interpreted by set-theoretic intersection. Hence the realizers of a universally quantified type
\forall X\sigma
are
\lambda
-terms which are, at the same time, realizers of all possible instantiations of
\sigma
.
This property plays an important role in the aforementioned completeness results. Moreover, due to this property, realizability yields one (among several existing) formalisations of Strachey’s informal notion of
parametric polymorphism

[
Str67
]
. Strachey notoriously made a distinction between
ad hoc
and
parametric
polymorphic programs, where the latter are, roughly, programs which behave in the same way for all possible type instantiations. A realizer of a universally quantified type is thus parametric in this informal sense, since the same term realizes all instantiations of the type. However, the relationship between realizability and other formalisations of parametricity is not yet completely understood.

1.1.
Contributions

In this paper we investigate completeness and parametricity for a general class of realizability models defined in terms of closure operators over sets of
\lambda
-terms
[
VM04
,
Rib08
]
.
In particular,
(1) we establish a general completeness theorem for positive types, which subsumes existing ones and applies to other semantics and
(2) we show that the closed realizers of a positive type satisfy other parametricity conditions given in terms of
logical relations
and
dinaturality
.
(1) and (2) allow to conclude that, for positive types, typability, realizability and parametricity are equivalent properties of closed normal
\lambda
-terms.

The models we consider include the semantics generally used to prove normalization theorems for type theories:
saturated sets
,
reducibility candidates
,
\beta
and
\beta\eta
-stable sets

[
Tai67
,
Gir72
,
Kri93
]
.
We do not consider categorical models, but we expect that
the techniques here presented might scale to a categorical setting (e.g. to the category of
modest sets
of
\lambda
-terms
[
Str91
]
).

1.1.1.
Completeness

The completeness problem is the one to show that, for a certain class
\mathcal{C}
of types, for any type
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}
, if
M
is a closed (normal) realizer of
\sigma
, then
M
provably has type
\sigma
.
Several completeness results have been established for simple types and positive types for semantics based on sets of
\lambda
-terms
[
Hin83a
,
Hin82
,
Hin83b
,
Kri93
,
FN98
]
. All these results use a similar method which consists in constructing a “term model” in which types are interpreted as sets of provably typable terms.
By considering the class of realizability semantics generated by a closure operator over sets of
\lambda
-terms, we provide a general completeness argument based on this method which subsumes the results mentioned. While Girard’s original reducibility candidates semantics seems to fall out of this approach, we show that our result applies to two variants of this semantics introduced in
[
Cou12
]
.

1.1.2.
Parametricity

We compare realizability semantics with the approaches to parametric polymorphism based on
logical relations

[
Rey83
]
and on
dinatural transformations

[
BFSS90
]
.

Logical relations are a standard semantic technique used to prove properties of programs (e.g.
program equivalence
or
representation independence
, see
[
DAB11
,
AJSW17
]
). The basic result on logical relations is a proof that
typable terms are
invariant
with respect to logical relations, that is, that they map related objects into related ones. This invariance property can be read as a parametricity condition for polymorphic programs, as it expresses the fact that the programs behave in related ways in related contexts.

We propose a formalisation of logical relations in the realizability semantics generated by closure operators.
Our construction relies on the demand that closure operators be
stable by union
(a property first investigated in
[
Rib07
]
for reducibility candidates). Under this condition, closed sets form a topology, and closed logical relations can be defined by the product topology.

Our main result is the
invariance theorem
(Theorem
71
) stating that closed realizers are invariant with respect to closed logical relations (under suitable conditions of the closure operator).
A consequence is that all realizability semantics used for normalization theorems satisfy the parametricity condition expressed by invariance with respect to
\beta
-stable and
\beta\eta
-stable logical relations.
The proof is obtained by adapting the “term model” technique used for completeness to the case of logical relations.
The invariance theorem generalizes a similar result proved in
[
Pis17
]
for the
\beta\eta
-stable semantics and restricted to simple types.

For second order types, the invariance property does not coincide with
Reynolds’ parametricity

[
Rey83
]
, which is based on a different interpretation of the universal quantifier.
We shortly discuss why, in order to account for Reynolds’ parametricity, one has to enrich closed sets of
\lambda
-terms with equivalence relations extending
\beta\eta
-equivalence (as in parametric
\mathsf{PER}
models,
[
BFSS90
]
).

We then consider the approach to parametricity through dinatural transformations
[
BFSS90
,
GSS92
]
. In categorical semantics, types can be interpreted as functors in which variables may occur both covariantly and contravariantly. Dinatural transformations generalize natural transformations to such functors
[
Mac78
]
.

In contrast with logical relations, dinaturality provides a purely equational criterion for parametricity, as it is expressed by a family of commuting diagrams.
We consider a syntactic approach to dinaturality in which commuting diagrams are replaced by
\beta
and
\beta\eta
-equations.

Syntactic dinaturality can be defined as dinaturality in the syntactic category generated by System
\mathsf{F}
terms (as in
[
dL09
]
).
We adopt here a more uniform definition of syntactic dinaturality using indeterminates, already used in
[
Pis17
]
.
This definition allows one to express dinaturality by a single equation, making this condition more amenable to syntactic treatment.
While a term satisfying this equational condition yields a dinatural transformation in the syntactic category, we do not know if the converse holds.

We prove that the closed realizers of a positive type are dinatural. This result is obtained by showing that invariant closed terms satisfy the syntactic dinaturality condition. We finally discuss some applications of this result, including a second argument to prove completeness for positive types.

1.2.
Related work

The two variants
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
of reducibility candidates here investigated come from
[
Cou12
]
, where a completeness problem of a different nature is considered for this semantics.
Moreover, we take from
[
Cou12
]
the remark that the “term model” method to prove completeness relies on a property of closure operators, that we call here
\mathsf{F}
-adaptedness
(Definition
4.1
).

Our analysis of logical relations relies on stability by union of closure operators. This property is established for reducibility candidates in
[
Rib07
,
Rib09
]
. Here we show that this property extends to the variants
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
, by adopting a similar proof method.

Connections between realizability and parametricity have been investigated in the literature at a formal level (for formal approaches to parametricity see
[
ACC93
]
,
[
Mai91
]
and
[
PA93
]
).
In
[
Wad07
]
realizability and parametricity are related as formal translations within a second order intuitionistic predicate calculus
P^{2}
. In particular, the realizers of inductive types are shown to correspond exactly to the parametric terms which satisfy the predicates expressing the so-called “Reynolds” translation of those types in
P^{2}
.
As a consequence, binary parametricity, as a formal translation, corresponds to the composition of unary parametricity and realizability. This idea is generalized in
[
BL11
]
, where formal realizability and parametricity are related in the case of Pure Type Systems, and it is shown that
n+1
-ary parametricity corresponds to
n
-ary parametricity composed with realizability. In other words, realizability is seen as a translation which increases arities of parametricity. In the concluding section we suggest that the extension of our treatment of realizability and parametricity to Krivine’s functional arithmetic
\mathcal{AF}2

[
Kri93
]
might allow to establish a similar connection between realizability and parametricity within our approach, as a consequence of the invariance theorem.

1.3.
Structure of the paper

The paper can be ideally divided in two parts: the first part (sections
2
,
3
) recalls some syntactic properties of System
\mathsf{F}
and introduces our general approach to realizability semantics by means of closure operators; the second part (sections
4
,
5
,
6
) relates realizability with completeness, logical relations and dinaturality.
In Section
2
, after recalling System
\mathsf{F}
and the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus (Subsection
2.1
), we introduce positive types and prove some syntactic results which relate them with simple types (Subsection
2.2
). In Section
3
we introduce the realizability semantics generated by a closure operator and we show how some well-known semantics which fit into our framework. In Section
4
we discuss completeness for positive types (Corollary
54
). In Section
5
we formalize
\mathfrak{C}
-closed logical relations (Subsection
5.1
) and we prove the
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance theorem (Theorem
71
) (Subsection
5.2
). Then we shortly discuss the problems to extend our approach to Reynolds’ parametricity (Subsection
5.3
).
In Section
6
we introduce syntactic dinaturality (Subsection
6.1
), we prove that
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant closed terms are syntactically dinatural (Subsection
6.2
), and we discuss applications of this result (Subsection
6.3
). In the concluding section (Section
7
), we put all results together to state the equivalence of realizability,
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance, dinaturality and typability for positive types (Theorem
88
) and we suggest some open problems and further directions.

To enhance readability, we enclose an index of defined terms at the end of the paper.

2.
System
\mathsf{F}
and positive types

We recall System
\mathsf{F}
and the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus (in the formulation
à la Curry
) and we
discuss the class of positive types, introduced in
[
Kri93
]
.
The main result of this section (Theorem
14
) allows to compare simple types and positive types and will be used in several places in the next sections to lift results from simple to positives types.

2.1.
\lambda_{\to}
and System
\mathsf{F}

2.1.1.
\lambda
-calculus

In the following, by the letters
M,N,P,\dots
we will indicate elements of the set
\Lambda
of untyped
\lambda
-terms, subject to usual
\alpha
-equivalence. By the letters
x,y,z,\dots
we will indicate
term variables
, i.e. the variables that might occur free or bound in untyped
\lambda
-terms. We let
\mathtt{TermVar}
indicate the set of term variables.
As usual, by the expression
M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}\dots M_{n}
we will indicate the term
(\dots((M_{1}M_{2})M_{3})\dots M_{n})
. For all
M\in\Lambda
,
\mathtt{FV}(M)
and
\mathtt{BV}(M)
indicate, respectively, the set of free and bound term variables occurring in
M
.

Given a reduction relation
R
, we will indicate by
\to_{R}
one-step
R
-reduction, by
\to_{R}^{*}
the transitive closure of
\to_{R}
and by
\simeq_{R}
the transitive-reflexive-symmetric closure of
\to_{R}
, i.e. the equivalence relation generated by
R
. Finally, by a
R
-normal
\lambda
-term we indicate a term to which no
R
-reduction can be applied.

In this paper we will consider the following reduction relations:
\beta
-reduction,
\eta
-reduction,
\beta\eta
-reduction and
wh
-reduction (weak-head reduction), where the latter is the reduction generated by

(\lambda x.P)QQ_{1}\dots Q_{n}\ \to_{wh}\ P[Q/x]Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}

(1)

We let
\mathcal{SN}
indicate the set of
\beta
-strongly normalizing

\lambda
-terms,
\mathcal{N}
indicate the set of
neutral
\lambda
-terms
, i.e. the
\lambda
-terms not beginning with a
\lambda
and
\mathcal{V}=\Lambda-\mathcal{N}
indicate the set of non-neutral
\lambda
-terms, that we call
values
. We let
\mathcal{N}^{*}
be the set of neutral non
\beta
-normal
\lambda
-terms.

Finally, for all
M\in\mathcal{SN}
, we let
\mathsf{d}(M)
, the
depth of
M
, be the maximum length of a reduction sequence from
M
to its
\beta
-normal form.

2.1.2.
System
\mathsf{F}

We recall the formulation “à la Curry” of System
\mathsf{F}
:

{defi}

[System
\mathsf{F}
‘‘à la Curry’’]

Given a countable set
\mathtt{TypeVar}=\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\dots\}
(that we will also write as
X,Y,Z,\dots
) of symbols, called
type variables
(or, simply, variables when no ambiguity occurs), the set
\mathtt{T}
of types of System
\mathsf{F}
is defined by the grammar below:

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}\>=\ X\mid\mathtt{T}\to\mathtt{T}\mid\forall X\mathtt{T}

(2)

Given
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,
\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)
and
\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)
indicate, respectively, the set of the
free
and
bound
type variables occurring in
\sigma
.

A
type declaration
is an expression of the form
x:\sigma
, where
x
is a term variable and
\sigma
is a type. A
context

\Gamma
is a finite set of type declarations in which distinct declarations have distinct variables. A
judgement
is an expression of the form
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
, where
\Gamma
is a context,
M
a term and
\sigma
a type.

The typing derivations of System
\mathsf{F}
are generated by the rules in figure
1
, in which
X
is bindable in
\Gamma
if, for any type declaration
x:\sigma\in\Gamma
,
X\notin\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)
.

\begin{matrix}\Gamma\vdash x:\sigma x:\sigma\in\Gamma&\\
\ &\ \\
\Gamma\vdash MN:\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\to\tau\Gamma\vdash N:\sigma&\Gamma-\{x:\sigma\}\vdash\lambda x.M:\sigma\to\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash M:\tau x:\sigma\in\Gamma\\
\ &\ \\
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma[\tau/X]\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\sigma&\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\sigma\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma X\text{ bindable in $\Gamma$}\\
\end{matrix}

Figure 1
.
System
\mathsf{F}
rules

We indicate by
\mathtt{T}_{0}
the set of
simple types
, i.e. those types
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
containing no occurrence of the quantifier
\forall
.
The simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus
\lambda_{\to}
is the subsystem of
\mathsf{F}
in which types are restricted to
\mathtt{T}_{0}
and rules are restricted to
(id),(\to E)
and
(\to I)
.

The set
S(\sigma)
of
subtypes
of a type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
is defined by induction as follows:

\begin{split}S(X)&:=\{X\}\\
S(\sigma\to\tau)&:=S(\sigma)\cup S(\tau)\cup\{\sigma\to\tau\}\\
S(\forall X\sigma)&:=S(\sigma)\cup\{\forall X\sigma\}\end{split}

(3)

{conv}

We adopt some conventions about the names of bound variables appearing in types.
We will suppose that in any type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, for any variable
X\in\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)
there is exactly one subtype of
\sigma
of the form
\forall X\tau
. Equivalently, that for any two distinct subtypes
\forall X\tau,\forall X^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}\in S(\sigma)
,
X\neq X^{\prime}
.
We will similarly suppose that in any type judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma_{n+1}
, where
\Gamma=\{x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\sigma_{n}\}
, for any variable
X\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathtt{BV}(\sigma_{i})
there is exactly one
1\leq j\leq n+1
and exactly one subtype of
S(\sigma_{j})
of the form
\forall X\tau
.

Given a type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
and a finite (possibly empty) list of pairwise distict type variables
\mathcal{X}=X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, by
\forall\mathcal{X}\sigma
we indicate the type
\forall X_{1}\dots\forall X_{n}\sigma
. We let

\mathtt{ListVar}
indicate the set of finite lists of pairwise distinct type variables.
For
\mathcal{X}\in\mathtt{ListVar}
, we indicate by
\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}
the set of type variables occurring in
\mathcal{X}
.

For any
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, by a position in
\sigma
we indicate a node in the syntactic tree of
\sigma
. A node is positive (resp. negative) if the unique path from the root to the node passes an even (resp. odd) number of times in the lefthand side of a
\to
-bifurcation.

We recall that System
\mathsf{F}

à la Curry
enjoys the
subject
\beta
-reduction
property: if
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
and
M\to_{\beta}^{*}M^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma
. On the contrary,
subject
\eta
-reduction
fails. For instance
\sigma=(\forall YX\to X)\to(X\to X)
,
\vdash\lambda x.\lambda y.xy:\sigma
holds but
\vdash\lambda x.x:\sigma
does not hold.

2.1.3.
Some useful terms

We introduce some further notations for some terms typable in System
\mathsf{F}
. Given terms
M,N
we let:

{\@index{\@indexphrase{System F}\@indexphrase{Terms $M\circ N$ and $\left(M\Rightarrow N\right)$}}}\begin{split}M\circ N&:=\lambda x.M(Nx)\\
\left(M\Rightarrow N\right)&:=\lambda x.\lambda y.N(x(My))\end{split}

(4)

The following properties are easily verified:

Lemma 1
.

(i)

If
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau\to\rho
and
\Gamma\vdash N:\sigma\to\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash M\circ N:\sigma\to\rho
.

(ii)

If
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\to\tau
and
\Gamma\vdash N:\sigma^{\prime}\to\tau^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\left(M\Rightarrow N\right):(\tau\to\sigma^{\prime})\to(\sigma\to\tau^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2
.

\left(P\Rightarrow Q\right)\circ\left(P^{\prime}\Rightarrow Q^{\prime}\right)\ \simeq_{\beta}\ \left(P^{\prime}\circ P\Rightarrow Q\circ Q^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof 2.1
.

A short computation shows that both terms
\beta
-reduce to

\lambda x.\lambda y.Q(Q^{\prime}(x(P^{\prime}(P(y)))))
.

For any
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, we define, by induction, a
\beta
-normal term
U_{\sigma}
with a unique free variable
x
such that
x:\sigma\vdash U_{\sigma}:\sigma
:

\begin{split}U_{X}&:=x\\
U_{\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}}&:=\lambda z.U_{\sigma_{2}}\left[xU_{\sigma_{1}}[z/x]/x\right]\\
U_{\forall X\sigma}&:=U_{\sigma}\end{split}

(5)

Proposition 3
.

For all
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,
x:\sigma\vdash U_{\sigma}:\sigma
is derivable and moreover
U_{\sigma}\to_{\eta}^{*}x
.

Proof 2.2
.

We argue by induction on
\sigma
. Both claims are obvious for
\sigma=X
. If
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}
, then, by induction hypothesis,

x:\sigma_{i}\vdash U_{\sigma_{i}}:\sigma_{i}
for
i=1,2
, so we deduce

x:\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2},z:\sigma_{1}\vdash U_{\sigma_{2}}[xU_{\sigma_{1}}[z/x]/x]:\sigma_{2}
, whence

x:\sigma\vdash U_{\sigma}:\sigma
. Moreover, by the induction hypothesis
U_{\sigma_{i}}\to^{*}_{\eta}x
, for
x=1,2
, whence

U_{\sigma}\to^{*}_{\eta}\lambda z.xz\to_{\eta}x
.
If
\sigma=\forall X\sigma^{\prime}
, by induction hypothesis,

x:\sigma^{\prime}\vdash U_{\sigma^{\prime}}:\sigma^{\prime}
; from
x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma^{\prime}
we deduce
x:\sigma\vdash U_{\sigma^{\prime}}:\sigma^{\prime}
, and we conclude
x:\sigma\vdash U_{\sigma^{\prime}}:\sigma
since
X
is not free in
\sigma
. That
U_{\sigma}\to^{*}_{\eta}x
is just the induction hypothesis.

For all
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, we let
I_{\sigma}=\lambda x.U_{\sigma}
. From Proposition
3
it follows that

\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma\to\sigma
and that
I_{\sigma}\to^{*}_{\eta}\lambda x.x
. Moreover we have the following two lemmas.

Lemma 4
.

If
M
is
\beta
-normal and
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
, then there exists
M^{\prime}

\beta
-normal such that
I_{\sigma}M\to_{\beta}^{*}M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\eta}M
.

Proof 2.3
.

We have
I_{\sigma}M\to_{\eta}^{*}(\lambda x.x)M
. Let
M^{\prime}
be the
\beta
-normal form of
I_{\sigma}M
. By the commutation property of
\beta\eta
-reduction
[
Bar85
, Lemma 3.3.8 p.66]
, there exists
P
such that
M^{\prime}\to_{\eta}^{*}P
and
(\lambda x.x)M\to_{\beta}^{*}P
. From
M^{\prime}\to_{\eta}^{*}P
we deduce that
P
is
\beta
-normal (as
\eta
-reduction does not create
\beta
-redexes), and since
(\lambda x.x)M\to_{\beta}^{*}P
and
M
is
\beta
-normal, it must be
P=M
.

Lemma 5
.

For all types
\sigma,\tau
:

(i)

\left(I_{\sigma}\Rightarrow I_{\tau}\right)\to_{\beta}^{*}I_{\sigma\to\tau}
.

(ii)

I_{\sigma}\circ I_{\sigma}\to_{\beta}^{*}I_{\sigma}
.

Proof 2.4
.

For point (i), we have

\left(I_{\sigma}\Rightarrow I_{\tau}\right)=\lambda x.\lambda z.I_{\tau}(x(I_{\sigma}z))\to_{\beta}^{*}\lambda x.\lambda z.U_{\tau}[xU_{\sigma}[z/x]/x]=I_{\sigma\to\tau}
.
For point (ii) we argue by induction on
\sigma
.
If
\sigma=X
, then
I_{\sigma}\circ I_{\sigma}=\lambda z.(\lambda y.y)((\lambda x.x)z)\to_{\beta}^{*}\lambda z.z=I_{\sigma}
.
If
\sigma=\tau\to\rho
, then by point (i) and Lemma
2

I_{\sigma}\circ I_{\sigma}\simeq_{\beta}\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right)\circ\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right)\simeq\left(I_{\tau}\circ I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\sigma}\circ I_{\sigma}\right)
, and by the induction hypothesis and point (i) we deduce
\left(I_{\tau}\circ I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\circ I_{\rho}\right)\simeq\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right)\simeq_{\beta}I_{\sigma}
.
Hence
I_{\sigma}\circ I_{\sigma}\simeq_{\beta}I_{\sigma}
, and since
I_{\sigma}
is
\beta
-normal, we conclude

I_{\sigma}\circ I_{\sigma}\to_{\beta}^{*}I_{\sigma}
.
Finally, for
\sigma=\forall X\tau
, the claim follows from the induction hypothesis as
I_{\sigma}=I_{\tau}
.

2.2.
Positive types

We introduce positive types
[
Kri93
]
and we compare typability with respect to such types and with respect to simple types.
We analyze the role of
\eta
-equivalence by distinguishing between
proper
and
improper
quantification (as in
[
FN98
]
).
We finally prove that the typability of a closed
\beta
-normal term
M
at a positive type
\sigma
is equivalent to the typability of
M
at a certain simple type, called the
skeleton
of
\sigma
, when all quantifications in
\sigma
are proper. If
\sigma
contains some improper quantification, then this equivalence holds only up to
\eta
-equivalence.

2.2.1.
Positive second order types

Positive second order types (introduced in
[
Kri93
]
) are defined by the restriction that the
\forall
quantifier can only occur in positive positions.

{defi}

[positive and negative types,
[
Kri93
]
]
The classes
\forall^{+},\forall^{-}
are defined inductively as follows:

•

X\in\forall^{+},\forall^{-}
;

•

if
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, and
\tau\in\forall^{+}
, then
\sigma\to\tau\in\forall^{+}
;

•

if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, and
\tau\in\forall^{-}
, then
\sigma\to\tau\in\forall^{-}
;

•

if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, then
\forall X\sigma\in\forall^{+}
.

It is easily checked that
\forall^{+}
is the class of types in which the
\forall
quantifier occurs only in positive positions and
\forall^{-}
is the class of types in which the
\forall
quantifier occurs only in negative positions.

Lemma 6
.

\forall^{+}\cap\forall^{-}=\mathtt{T}_{0}
.

Proof 2.5
.

That
\mathtt{T}_{0}\subseteq\forall^{+}\cap\forall^{-}
is easily verified by induction on
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
.
For the converse direction, if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}\cap\forall^{-}
then if a quantifier occured in
\sigma
, it would occur in a position both positive and negative, which is impossible. Therefore we can conclude
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
.

Lemma 7
.

If
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, then
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\dots\to\sigma_{p}\to Y
, for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
, variable
Y
and types
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{p}\in\forall^{+}
.

Proof 2.6
.

By induction on
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
: if
\sigma=X
, then the claim obviously holds; otherwise, it must be
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}
, where
\sigma_{1}\in\forall^{+}
and
\sigma_{2}\in\forall^{-}
, and by induction hypothesis

\sigma_{2}=\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{q}\to Y
, for some variable
Y
and types
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{2}\in\forall^{+}
, so
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{q}\to Y
satisfies the claim.

To compare positive types and simple types it is useful to consider a subclass of
\forall^{+}
(as well as a subclass of
\forall^{-}
) defined as follows:

{defi}

[
\mathbf{\Pi},\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}
-types]
Let
\sigma
be a type.

(i)

\sigma\in\mathbf{\Pi}
if for some
\mathcal{X}\in\mathtt{ListVar}
and some
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
,
\sigma=\forall\mathcal{X}\sigma^{\prime}
;

(ii)

\sigma\in\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}
if for some
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{p}\in\mathbf{\Pi}
and a variable
X
,
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\dots\to\sigma_{p}\to X
.

It is clear that
\mathbf{\Pi}\subseteq\forall^{+}
and
\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}\subseteq\forall^{-}
, but the converse inclusions do not hold.
For instance, the type
\forall Y((\forall XX\to Y)\to Y)
is in
\forall^{+}
but is not in
\mathbf{\Pi}
.

2.2.2.
The skeleton

Given a type
\sigma
and a set
S\subseteq\mathtt{TypeVar}
of variables not occurring free in
\sigma
, we can define the type
\sigma^{S}
obtained by
deleting from
\sigma
all quantifiers of the form
\forall X
, for all
X
occurring in
S
. More formally:

{defi}

Let
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
and let
S\subseteq\mathtt{TypeVar}
be such that
S\cap\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)=\emptyset
. We define the type
\sigma^{S}
as follows:

\begin{split}X^{S}&:=X\\
(\tau\to\rho)^{S}&:=\tau^{S}\to\rho^{S}\\
(\forall X\tau)^{S}&:=\begin{cases}\tau^{S}&\text{ if }X\in S\\
\forall X\tau^{S}&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}\end{split}

(6)

The
skeleton

\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
of a type
\sigma
is the simple type obtained from
\sigma
by deleting all quantifiers.

{defi}

[skeleton]
For all
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, we let
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma):=\sigma^{\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)}\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
.

From Definition
2.2.2
it is clear that
\mathsf{sk}(X)=X
,

\mathsf{sk}(\sigma\to\tau)=\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau)
and
\mathsf{sk}(\forall X\sigma)=\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
.
Given a class of types
\mathcal{C}
and a context
\Gamma
, by the expression
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
we indicate that, for any type
\sigma
occurring in some type declaration in
\Gamma
,
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}
. Also, given
\Gamma=\{x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\sigma_{n}\}
by
\mathsf{sk}(\Gamma)
we indicate the context
\{x_{1}:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma_{1}),\dots,x_{n}:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma_{n})\}
.

In the following we will consider derivations
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
, where
\Gamma\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
. These derivations satisfy the property below, which is proved in Appendix
A

Proposition 8
.

Suppose
M
is
\beta
-normal and
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
is derivable, where
\Gamma\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
. Then there exists a derivation of
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
which does not employ the rule
\forall
E and such that, for any type judgement
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime}
occurring in it,
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\forall^{+}
.

From Proposition
8
we deduce the following property of skeletons:

Lemma 9
.

Let
M
be a
\beta
-normal
\lambda
-term, such that
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
, where
\Gamma\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
. Then
\mathsf{sk}(\Gamma)\vdash M:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
.

Proof 2.7
.

We argue by induction on a typing derivation which does not use the rule
\forall
E (by using Proposition
8
):

•

if the derivation is
x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma
, then
\sigma\in\forall^{-}\cap\forall^{+}
, hence
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
(by Lemma
6
) and
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)=\sigma
, so the claim trivially holds;

•

If the derivation ends by

\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:\sigma\to\tau

then, since
\sigma\to\tau\in\forall^{+}
,
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
and
\tau\in\forall^{+}
; by the induction hypothesis, then
\mathsf{sk}(\Gamma),x:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\vdash M:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)
, whence the claim follows from
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma\to\tau)=\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau)
;

•

If the derivation ends by
\Gamma\vdash xM_{1}\dots M_{p}:\sigma
, with
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:\tau_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,p
; then
\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{p}\to\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, hence,
\tau_{i}\in\forall^{+}
and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}\cap\forall^{-}
must be a simple type. By the induction hypothesis then
\mathsf{sk}(\Gamma)\vdash M_{i}:\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{i})
, hence the claim follows from
\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{p}\to\sigma)=\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{1})\to\dots\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{p})\to\sigma
;

•

If the derivation ends by

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma

\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\sigma

, where
X\notin\mathtt{FV}(\Gamma)
, then by the induction hypothesis
\mathsf{sk}(\Gamma)\vdash M:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
and the claim follows from
\mathsf{sk}(\forall X\sigma)=\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
.

2.2.3.
A translation from
\mathbf{\Pi}
to
\forall^{+}

A first step to compare positive and simple types comes from a comparison between the classes
\forall^{+}
and
\mathbf{\Pi}
. To this end we introduce translations
[\_]^{+}:\forall^{+}\to\mathbf{\Pi}
and
[\_]^{-}:\forall^{-}\to\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}
as follows:

{defi}

[positive and negative translations]
If
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, we let

\sigma^{+}:=\forall\mathcal{X}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\in\mathbf{\Pi}

where
\mathcal{X}
is any list such that
\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)
.
If
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, we let

\sigma^{-}:=\forall\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma_{1})\to\dots\to\forall\mathcal{X}_{p}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma_{p})\to Y\in\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}

where

\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\dots\to\sigma_{p}\to Y
(by Lemma
7
) and,
for
i=1,\dots,p
,
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is any list such that
\mathcal{X}_{i}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{BV}(\sigma_{i})
.

The following lemma provides a useful characterization of the types
\sigma^{+}
and
\sigma^{-}
:

Lemma 10
.

For any type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, the following hold:

•

if
\sigma=X
, then
X^{+}=X
(resp.
X^{-}=X
);

•

if
\sigma=\tau\to\rho\in\forall^{-}
, then
\sigma^{-}=\tau^{+}\to\rho^{-}
;

•

if
\sigma=\tau\to\rho\in\forall^{+}
, then

(\tau\to\rho)^{+}=\forall\mathcal{X}((\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{1})\to\dots\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{p})\to Y)\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho))

where
\tau=\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{p}\to Y
and
\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{BV}(\tau_{1})\cup\dots\cup\mathtt{BV}(\tau_{n})\cup\mathtt{BV}(\rho)
.

•

if
\sigma=\forall X\tau
, then
\sigma^{+}=\forall X\tau^{+}
.

Proof 2.8
.

We argue by induction on
\sigma
.
The first case is clear. Suppose
\sigma=\tau\to\rho\in\forall^{-}
, hence
\tau\in\forall^{+}
and
\rho\in\forall^{-}
. By
Lemma
7

\rho=\rho_{1}\to\dots\to\rho_{p}\to Y
, where
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{p}\in\forall^{+}
.
Then
\sigma^{-}=\forall\mathcal{X}\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\forall\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathsf{sk}(\rho_{1})\to\dots\to\forall\mathcal{X}_{p}\mathsf{sk}(\rho_{p})\to Y
, where
\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{BV}(\tau)
and
\mathcal{X}_{i}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{BV}(\rho_{i})
, that is,
\sigma^{-}=\tau^{+}\to\rho^{-}
.
Suppose now
\sigma=\tau\to\rho\in\forall^{+}
, hence
\tau\in\forall^{-}
and
\rho\in\forall^{+}
. By Lemma
7
,
\tau=\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{p}\to Y
, where
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p}\in\forall^{+}
. Then

\sigma^{+}=\forall\mathcal{X}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)=\forall\mathcal{X}((\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{1})\to\dots\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau_{p})\to Y)\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho))
.
Finally, if
\sigma=\forall X\tau
, then
\sigma^{+}=\forall\mathcal{X}\mathsf{sk}(\tau)=\forall X(\forall\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathsf{sk}(\tau))=\forall X\tau^{+}
, where
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{X}-\{X\}
.

Lemma 11
.

For any type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,

(i)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, then
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma\to\sigma^{+}
;

(ii)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, then
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma^{-}\to\sigma
.

Proof 2.9
.

We first prove the following fact: if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
(resp.
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
), then
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma^{+}\to\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
(resp.
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\to\sigma^{-}
).
If
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, then the claim follows from the fact that
\sigma^{+}
is of the form
\forall\mathcal{X}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
.
If
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, we argue by induction on
\sigma
: if
\sigma=X
the claim clearly holds; if
\sigma=\tau\to\rho
, then from the induction hypothesis
\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau^{+}\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau)
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\mathsf{sk}(\rho)\to\rho^{-}
, whence
\vdash\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right):(\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho))\to\tau^{+}\to\rho^{-}
and, since
\mathsf{sk}(\tau\to\rho)=\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)
and

(\tau\to\rho)^{-}=\tau^{+}\to\rho^{-}
, we can conclude by Lemma
5
(i) and subject
\beta
-reduction.

We can now prove claims (i),(ii) by induction on
\sigma
. The case
\sigma=X
is trivial. Let
\sigma=\tau\to\rho
.
If
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, then
\tau\in\forall^{-}
and
\rho\in\forall^{+}
.
By what we just proved and the induction hypothesis we have
\vdash I_{\tau}:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\tau^{-},\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau^{-}\to\tau
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\rho^{+}\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\rho\to\rho^{+}
. By Lemma
5
(ii),
I_{\tau}\circ I_{\tau}\to_{\beta}^{*}I_{\tau}
and
I_{\rho}\circ I_{\rho}\to_{\beta}^{*}I_{\rho}
, hence by subject
\beta
-reduction we deduce

\vdash I_{\tau}:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\tau
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\rho\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)
.
We deduce then
\vdash\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right):\sigma\to\sigma^{+}
as illustrated in fig.
2
and we conclude

\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma\to\sigma^{+}
by Lemma
5
(i) and subject
\beta
-reduction.

\vdash I_{\rho}:\rho\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)

x:\sigma\vdash x:\tau\to\rho

\vdash I_{\tau}:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\tau

y:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\vdash y:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)

y:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\vdash I_{\tau}y:\tau

x:\sigma,y:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\vdash x(I_{\tau}y):\rho

x:\sigma,y:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\vdash I_{\rho}(x(I_{\tau}y)):\mathsf{sk}(\rho)

\vdash\lambda x.\lambda y.I_{\rho}(x(I_{\tau}y)):\sigma\to\sigma^{+}

Figure 2
.
Derivation of
\vdash\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right):\sigma\to\sigma^{+}
, for
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
.

One can argue similarly for
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
. Finally, if
\sigma=\forall X\tau
, by induction hypothesis
\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau^{+}\to\tau
, from which one easily deduces
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma^{+}\to\sigma
.

2.2.4.
Proper positive types

In order to investigate in more detail the relation between positive and
\mathbf{\Pi}
types, we must refine our description of quantification. In particular, we must distinguish, as in
[
FN98
]
, between
proper
and
improper
quantification.

Given a type
\sigma
, for any bound variable
X\in\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)
there exists a unique subtype
\sigma_{X}
of
\sigma
such that
\forall X\sigma_{X}
is a subtype of
\sigma
1
1

1

We are here exploiting Convention
2.1.2
on the name of bound variables.
.

{defi}

[proper quantification]
Given a type
\sigma
and a variable
X\in\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)
, we call
X

proper
if
X\in\mathtt{FV}(\sigma_{X})
and
improper
if
X\notin\mathtt{FV}(\sigma_{X})
.

We let
\mathtt{PV}(\sigma)
indicate the set of proper bound variables of
\sigma
and
\mathtt{IV}(\sigma)
indicate the set of improper bound variables of
\sigma
.

We call a type
\sigma

proper
if
\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)=\mathtt{PV}(\sigma)
and
strongly improper
if
\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)=\mathtt{IV}(\sigma)
.

For example, the type
\forall Y(\forall X(X\to Y)\to Y)
is proper and
the type
\forall Y(\forall X(Z\to Z)\to Z)
is strongly improper. A type which is both proper and strongly improper must be a simple type.

{defi}

[
\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}},\forall^{-}_{\mathtt{P}}
types,
[
FN98
]
]

\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
(resp.
\sigma\in\forall^{-}_{\mathtt{P}}
) if
\sigma
is proper and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
(resp.
\sigma
is proper and
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
).

We will see that the distinction between the classes
\forall^{+},\forall^{-}
and
\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}},\forall^{-}_{\mathtt{P}}
is related to the failure of the subject
\eta
-reduction property.

The reverse of Lemma
11
can be established for strongly improper types:

Lemma 12
.

For any type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,

(i)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
and is strongly improper, then
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma^{+}\to\sigma
;

(ii)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
and is strongly improper, then
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma\to\sigma^{-}
.

Proof 2.10
.

We first prove the following fact: if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
(resp.
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
) is strongly improper, then
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\to\sigma^{+}
(resp.
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma^{-}\to\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
).
For
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
the claim follows from the fact that
\sigma^{+}
is of the form
\forall\mathcal{X}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}\cap\mathtt{FV}(\mathsf{sk}(\sigma))=\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)\cap\mathtt{FV}(\mathsf{sk}(\sigma))=\emptyset
.
If
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, we argue by induction on
\sigma
: if
\sigma=X
it is trivial; if
\sigma=\tau\to\rho
, then the claim follows from the induction hypothesis and the definition of
I_{\sigma}
: from
\vdash I_{\tau}:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\tau^{+}
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\rho^{-}\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)
, it follows
\vdash\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right):(\tau^{+}\to\rho^{-})\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)
and, since

\mathsf{sk}(\tau\to\rho)=\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)
and
(\tau\to\rho)^{-}=\tau^{+}\to\rho^{-}
, we can conclude by Lemma
5
(i) and subject
\beta
-reduction.

We can now prove claims (i),(ii) by induction on
\sigma
. The case
\sigma=X
is trivial. Let
\sigma=\tau\to\rho
.
If
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, then, by what we just proved and the induction hypothesis, we have
\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau^{-}\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau),\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau\to\tau^{-}
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\mathsf{sk}(\rho)\to\rho^{+}
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\rho^{+}\to\rho
. By Lemma
5
(ii),
I_{\tau}\circ I_{\tau}\to_{\beta}^{*}I_{\tau}
and
I_{\rho}\circ I_{\rho}\to_{\beta}^{*}I_{\rho}
, hence by subject
\beta
-reduction we deduce

\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau)
and
\vdash I_{\rho}:\mathsf{sk}(\rho)\to\rho
. We deduce then
\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right):\sigma^{+}\to\sigma
as illustrated in fig.
3
and we conclude
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma^{+}\to\sigma
by Lemma
5
(i) and subject
\beta
-reduction.

\vdash I_{\rho}:\mathsf{sk}(\rho)\to\rho

x:\sigma^{+}\vdash x:\sigma^{+}

x:\sigma^{+}\vdash x:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)\to\mathsf{sk}(\rho)

\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau\to\mathsf{sk}(\tau)

y:\tau\vdash y:\tau

y:\tau\vdash I_{\tau}y:\mathsf{sk}(\tau)

x:\sigma^{+},y:\tau\vdash x(I_{\tau}y):\mathsf{sk}(\rho)

x:\sigma^{+},y:\tau\ \vdash I_{\rho}(x(I_{\tau}y)):\rho

\vdash\lambda x.\lambda y.I_{\rho}(x(I_{\tau}y)):\sigma^{+}\to\sigma

Figure 3
.
Derivation of
\vdash\left(I_{\tau}\Rightarrow I_{\rho}\right):\sigma^{+}\to\sigma
, for
\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
.

One can argue similarly for the case
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
. Finally, if
\sigma=\forall X\tau
, by induction hypothesis
\vdash I_{\tau}:\tau\to\tau^{+}
, from which one deduces
\vdash I_{\sigma}:\sigma\to\sigma^{+}
.

We now prove an important property of proper positive types.

Lemma 13
.

Let
M
be a
\beta
-normal
\lambda
-term, let
\Gamma,\Delta\in\forall^{-}_{\mathtt{P}}
and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
, and let
S\subseteq\mathtt{PV}(\sigma)\cup\mathtt{PV}(\Delta)
such that no variable in
S
occurs free in
\Gamma
.
If
\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash M:\sigma^{S}
, then
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M:\sigma
.

Proof 2.11
.

We argue by induction on a derivation
D
of
\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash M:\sigma^{S}
which, by Proposition
8
, does not use the
\forall
E rule and is such that, for any type declaration
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime}
in
D
,
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\forall^{+}
.

If
D
only consists of an axiom

x:\sigma^{S}\vdash x:\sigma^{S}
, then either
\Gamma=\{x:\sigma^{S}\}
and
\Delta=\emptyset
or
\Gamma=\emptyset
and
\Delta=\{x:\sigma\}
. In the first case, since
\mathtt{FV}(\Gamma)\cap S=\emptyset
, no variable in
S
can occur in
\sigma^{S}
. But, since

\sigma
is proper, this implies that
S=\emptyset
, hence
\sigma=\sigma^{S}
and the claim trivially holds.
In the second case, we have
x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma
.

If
D
ends by a
\to
I rule, then
\sigma^{S}=\tau\to\rho
. This implies that
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}
for some
\sigma_{1}\in\forall^{-}_{\mathtt{P}}
and
\sigma_{2}\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
and
\tau=\sigma_{1}^{S}
,
\rho=\sigma_{2}^{S}
.
D
is of the form

D\quad=\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 19.68855pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 40.56032pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$D^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S},x:\tau^{S}\vdash M^{\prime}:\rho^{S}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip-1.07036pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to100.48312pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 3.07034pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\to$I}}\vskip-1.07034pt\hbox{\hskip 6.06024pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash\lambda x.M^{\prime}:\sigma^{S}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces

By the induction hypothesis we have then
\Gamma,\Delta,x:\tau\vdash M^{\prime}:\rho
, from which we deduce
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M:\sigma
by
\to
I.

If
D
ends by a
\forall
I rule, then
\sigma^{S}=\forall Y\tau
. This implies that
Y\notin S
, that
Y
does not occur free in
\Gamma
and
\Delta
and that
\sigma=\forall Y\sigma_{1}
for some
\sigma_{1}\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
such that
\tau=\sigma_{1}^{S}
.
D
is of the form

D\quad=\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 19.78856pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 26.48962pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$D^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash M:\sigma_{1}^{S}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to72.34175pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\forall$I}}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash M:\sigma^{S}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces

By the induction hypothesis we have then
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M:\tau
, from which we deduce
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M:\sigma
by
\forall
I.

If
D
ends by a chain of applications of
\to
E rules, then
D
is of the form

D\quad=\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 22.75891pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 49.37364pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$D^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash z:\xi_{1}\to\dots\to\xi_{p}\to\sigma^{S}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 25.18053pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$D_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash M_{1}:\xi_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\dots$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 25.24863pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$D_{p}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash M_{p}:\xi_{p}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-1.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to320.05174pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\hss}\raise 2.0pt\hbox to320.05174pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}}\lower-1.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 123.855pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,\Delta^{S}\vdash M:\sigma^{S}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces

for some
p\geq 1
and types
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{p}\in\forall^{-}_{\mathtt{P}}
.
We must consider two cases:

(1)

if
z:\xi_{1}\to\dots\to\xi_{p}\to\sigma^{S}
occurs in
\Gamma
, then, since
\mathtt{FV}(\Gamma)\cap S=\emptyset
, no variable in
S
can occur either in
\sigma^{S}
nor in
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{p}
. But, since
\sigma
is proper, this implies that
S\subseteq\mathtt{BV}(\Delta)
,
\sigma=\sigma^{S}
and
\xi_{i}=\xi_{i}^{S}
, for
i=1,\dots,p
. From
\Gamma\in\forall^{-}_{\mathtt{P}}
it follows that
\xi_{i}\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
, for
i=1,\dots,p
. Hence
\xi_{i}=\xi_{i}^{S}
, for
i=1,\dots,p
, so we deduce, by the induction hypothesis, that
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M_{i}:\xi_{i}
, and we can conclude
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M:\sigma
.

(2)

if
z:\xi_{1}\to\dots\to\xi_{p}\to\sigma^{S}
occurs in
\Delta^{S}
, then there exist types
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{p}\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
such that
\xi_{1}=\eta_{1}^{S},\dots,\xi_{p}=\eta_{p}^{S}
and
z:\eta_{1}\to\dots\to\eta_{p}\to\sigma\in\Delta
. By the induction hypothesis we deduce then

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash z:\eta_{1}\to\dots\to\eta_{p}\to\sigma
and

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M_{i}:\eta_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,p
, from which we can conclude
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M:\sigma
.

2.2.5.
Comparison of simple and positive types

We now establish our main result on the comparison of positive and simple types.

Theorem 14
.

Let
M
be a closed
\beta
-normal term.

(i)

For all
\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
, if
\vdash M:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
then
\vdash M:\sigma
;

(ii)

For all
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, if
\vdash M:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
then
\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma
for some
M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\to_{\eta}^{*}M
.

Proof 2.12
.

For case (i), let
\mathcal{X}
be such that
\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)
; then
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)=\sigma^{\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}}
. By Lemma
13
we deduce then
\vdash M:\sigma
.

For case (ii),
the set
\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)
splits as the disjoint union of
\mathtt{PV}(\sigma)
and
\mathtt{IV}(\sigma)
.
Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
be such tha
\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{PV}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{{\dagger}}=\mathtt{IV}(\sigma)
.
From
\vdash M:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
we deduce
\vdash M:\forall\mathcal{Y}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
.
Now, since
\forall\mathcal{Y}\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)=(\sigma^{\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}})^{+}
, by Lemma
12
we deduce

\Gamma\vdash I_{\sigma^{\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}}}M:\sigma^{\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}}
and by Lemma
4
we deduce that
I_{\sigma^{\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}}}M\to_{\beta}^{*}M^{\prime}
, for some (obviously closed)
M^{\prime}

\beta
-normal such that
M^{\prime}\to_{\eta}^{*}M
, whence
\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma^{\mathcal{X}^{{\dagger}}}
holds by subject
\beta
-reduction. We can then conclude by Lemma
13
that
\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma
.

Remark 15
.

By putting together Theorem
14
and Lemma
9
we deduce that, for
\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
, typability at
\sigma
is equivalent to typability at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
for a closed
\beta
-normal term; for
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, typability at
\sigma
is equivalent to typability at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
for a closed
\beta
-normal term up to
\eta
-equivalence.

{exa}

Theorem
14
highlights how the distinction between proper and non proper types is related to
\eta
-expansion.
Let
\sigma=((\forall YX)\to X)\to(X\to X)\in\forall^{+}
. Then
\sigma^{+}=\forall Y((X\to X)\to(X\to X))
. While we have
\vdash\lambda x.x:\sigma^{+}
, it is not true that
\vdash\lambda x.x:\sigma
, but only
\vdash\lambda x.\lambda y.xy:\sigma
.

3.
Realizability semantics by closure operators

We introduce realizability semantics by means of closure operators over sets of
\lambda
-terms.
Following
[
Rib07
]
, we show that, if the closure operator is stable by union, then it generates a realizability semantics which is a topological space. This topological intuition is at the heart of several constructions in the following sections.

We prove a soundness theorem for a class of closure operators (called
adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operators
). This result
combines two different standard soundness theorems for realizability semantics: the one for the semantics based on sets of
\lambda
-terms (e.g.
\beta
-stable,
\beta\eta
-stable
and
\beta
-saturated sets
,
[
Kri93
]
) and the one for the semantics based on sets of strongly normalizing
\lambda
-terms (e.g.
reducibility candidates
). The latter is the fundamental ingredient to prove the strong normalization theorem for System F, and is based on the definition of a semantics made of closed sets containing all term variables. We will describe such semantics by means of
regular
closure operators.

We describe then how the above mentioned semantics fit into this general framework. Moreover, we introduce two variants of Girard’s reducibility candidates due to
[
Cou12
]
, which are more adapted to investigate completeness and parametricity.

3.1.
Semantics by closure operators

3.1.1.
Closure operators

We first recall the definition of closure operators. Then we introduce a class of closure operators over sets of
\lambda
-terms well-suited to interpret System
\mathsf{F}
.

{defi}

[closure operator]
A
closure operator

\mathfrak{C}
over a set
L
is a map
\mathfrak{C}:\wp({L})\to\wp({L})
such that, for all
s,t\in\wp({L})
,

(1)

s\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(s)
;

(2)

s\subseteq t\ \Rightarrow\ \mathfrak{C}(s)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(t)
;

(3)

\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{C}(s))=\mathfrak{C}(s)
.

Any closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L\subseteq\Lambda
generates a family of closed subsets of
L
, which are the
s\subseteq L
such that
\mathfrak{C}(s)=s
.
For any closure operator, the intersection of closed sets is closed:

Lemma 16
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a closure operator over
L\subseteq\Lambda
. Let
I\neq\emptyset
and
u_{i}\in\wp(L)
for all
i\in I
.
If all
u_{i}
are closed, then

\bigcap_{i\in I}u_{i}
is closed.

Proof 3.1
.

Let
u=\bigcap_{i\in I}u_{i}
. It suffices to show that
\mathfrak{C}(u)\subseteq u
. From
u\subseteq u_{i}
, we deduce
\mathfrak{C}(u)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(u_{i})=u_{i}
, for any
i\in I
, whence
\mathfrak{C}(u)\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}u_{i}=u
.

By Lemma
16
, to interpret System
\mathsf{F}
connectives it suffices that closed sets are stable with respect to the following construction:

{defi}

Given sets
s,t\subseteq\Lambda
, we let
s\to t=\{M\mid\forall P\ (P\in s\ \Rightarrow\ MP\in t)\}
.
Moreover, if
L\subseteq\Lambda
, for all
s,t\subseteq\Lambda
, we let
s\to_{L}t=(s\to t)\cap L
.

This leads to the following definition.

{defi}

[
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator]

A closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over a set
L
generates a family of closed sets
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\{s\in\wp({L})\mid\mathfrak{C}(s)=s\}\subseteq\wp({L})
.

A closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L\subseteq\Lambda
is a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
if for all
s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
s\to_{L}t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
If
\mathfrak{C}
is a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L
, the family
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is called a
semantics over
L
.

Two closure operator can be compared as follows:

{defi}

Let
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{D}
be closure operators over sets
L,L^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
. We say that
\mathfrak{C}
is
finer
than
{\mathfrak{D}}
, noted
{\mathfrak{C}}\leq{\mathfrak{D}}
, when, for any
s\subseteq L\cap L^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{C}(s)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}(s)
.

The following proposition establishes that, when
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{D}
are closure operators over the same set
L\subseteq\Lambda
, then the property
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
corresponds to the inclusion of the associated semantics.

Proposition 17
.

Let
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{D}
be two
\mathsf{F}
-closure operators over
L,L^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
, respectively. If
L\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
, then
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
iff
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}\cap L\subseteq\mathcal{\mathcal{}}S_{\mathfrak{C}}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}\cap L=\{s\cap L\mid s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}\}
.

Proof 3.2
.

First observe that, if
L\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
, then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}\cap L\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
, since for any
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
,
s\cap L\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
, by Lemma
16
. Moreover, since
L\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
, if
s\subseteq L
, then
\mathfrak{D}(s)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}(L)=L
.
Now, for the right-to-left direction, suppose
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}\cap L\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and let
s\subseteq L\cap L^{\prime}
. Then
\mathfrak{D}(s)\cap L=\mathfrak{D}(s)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, and we get
\mathfrak{C}(s)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{D}(s))=\mathfrak{D}(s)
.
For the left-to-right direction, suppose
\mathfrak{C}(s)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}(s)
for all
s\subseteq L\cap L^{\prime}
and let
t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}\cap L
, i.e.
t=t^{\prime}\cap L
for some
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
. Then
t\subseteq L\cap L^{\prime}
, hence

\mathfrak{C}(t)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}(t)=t
, i.e.
\mathfrak{C}(t)\subseteq t
; since
t\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(t)
, we deduce
\mathfrak{C}(t)=t
, i.e.
t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

We introduce now an important class of
\mathsf{F}
-closure operators:

{defi}

A closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
is
regular
when
\mathtt{TermVar}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)
.

The notion of regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operators captures a property which is often used in normalization proofs and that we will exploit to prove soundness (Theorem
28
) in the case in which the closure operator is defined over a subset of
\Lambda
(e.g. on
\mathcal{SN}
). In normalization arguments one usually considers a family of closed sets which contain all terms of the form
xP_{1}\dots P_{n}
, where the
P_{i}
are normalizing terms (see
[
Gal90
,
Kri93
]
).
This construction corresponds to defining a regular closure operator, as we now show.

For any set
L\subseteq\Lambda
, we let
L^{*}
be the set of all terms of the form
xP_{1}\dots P_{n}
, for some term variable
x
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and terms
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}\in L
.

Proposition 18
.

Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
. If
\mathfrak{C}
is a regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L
, then
L^{*}\subseteq L
and for all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
L^{*}\subseteq s
.

Proof 3.3
.

We show that for all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, variable
x
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and terms
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}\in L
,
xP_{1}\dots P_{n}\in s
. We argue by induction on
n
. For all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, from
\emptyset\subseteq s
, it follows
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(s)=s
. Since
\mathfrak{C}
is regular, then
\mathtt{TermVar}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq s
. This proves the case
n=0
.
For
n=k+1
, by the induction hypothesis for all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
P_{1},\dots,P_{k}\in L
,
xP_{1}\dots P_{k}\in s
. Let
P\in L
;
since
\mathfrak{C}(\{P\})\to_{L}s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, then
xP_{1}\dots P_{k}\in\mathfrak{C}(\{P\})\to_{L}s
, and this implies that
xP_{1}\dots P_{k}P\in~s
.

In Subsection
3.2
we will show that the reducibility candidates semantics is generated by a regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator.

3.1.2.
Stability by union

Stability by union is the property that arbitrary unions of closed sets are closed. This property was investigated in
[
Rib07
]
in the case of reducibility candidates. Following
[
Rib07
]
, we show that if a closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
is stable by union, then the
\mathfrak{C}
-closed sets (plus the empty set) form a topology. The topological presentation of a semantics will be exploited throughout the following sections.
In Subsection
3.2
and Appendix
B
we will show that all concrete semantics discussed in this paper are stable by union.

{defi}

Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
and
\mathfrak{C}
be a closure operator over
L
.
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies
stability by union
(
\mathsf{SU}
) if
for all
s\subseteq L
, if
s\neq\emptyset
,
\mathfrak{C}(s)=\bigcup_{P\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{P\})
.

Given a closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over a set
L
, let
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}=\{s\subseteq L\mid s\neq\emptyset\ and\ s=\bigcup_{x\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})\}\cup\{\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\}
.
We show that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\overline{{\mathfrak{C}}}
iff
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is closed with respect to arbitrary unions (we essentially follow
[
Rib07
]
, Prop. 4.1, p. 6).

Proposition 19
.

For any closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
,
\overline{{\mathfrak{C}}}
is the smallest set such that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
and for all non-empty
X\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
,
\bigcup X,\bigcap X\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Proof 3.4
.

For all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, if
s\neq\emptyset
,
s=\bigcup_{x\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})
. Indeed,
s\subseteq\bigcup_{x\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})
and for all
x\in s
,
\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(s)=s
. We can conclude then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Let now
X\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
be a non-empty set. Then for all
s\in X
,
s=\bigcup_{x\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})
, hence
\bigcup X=\bigcup_{x\in\bigcup X}\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})
, so
\bigcup X\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
. Moreover, if
x\in\bigcap X
, then
x\in s
, for all
s\in X
, hence
\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})\subseteq s
, since
s\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
, and we can conclude then
\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})\subseteq\bigcap X
. This implies that
\bigcap X=\bigcup_{x\in\bigcap X}\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})
, whence
\bigcap X\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
. If
\bigcap X=\emptyset
, observe first that, for all
s\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
,
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq s
. Indeed, either
s=\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)
, so the claim is trivial, or
s\neq\emptyset
. In this last case there is some
x\in s
, hence from
\emptyset\subseteq\{x\}
, we deduce
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})\subseteq s
.
Now, since
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq s
, for all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and
X
is non empty,
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq\bigcap X
, so it must be
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
, and we conclude again
\bigcap X\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Let now
S\subseteq\wp(L)
be such that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq S
and for all
X\subseteq S
non empty,
\bigcap X,\bigcup X\in S
.
If
s\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
, then either
s=\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)
or
s\neq\emptyset
and
s=\bigcup_{x\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})
. In the first, case, since
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq S
,
s\in S
; in the second case, since
X=\{\mathfrak{C}(\{x\})\mid x\in s\}\subseteq S
, we have
s=\bigcup X\in S
. This shows that
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq S
.

Corollary 20
.

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\overline{{\mathfrak{C}}}
iff for all
X\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
non-empty,
\bigcup X\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Proof 3.5
.

If
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
, then the claim follows from Proposition
19
. Conversely, if for all
X\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
non-empty,
\bigcup X\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, since for all
X\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
non-empty,
\bigcap X\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
by Lemma
16
, and trivially
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, by Proposition
19
,
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
, that is,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
.

\overline{{\mathfrak{C}}}\cup\{\emptyset\}
is the topological closure of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
. The pair
(L,\overline{\mathfrak{C}}\cup\{\emptyset\})
forms indeed a topological space, as it contains
L,\emptyset
and is closed by arbitrary unions and intersections. Observe that the addition of
\emptyset
is necessary to obtain a topology when
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\neq\emptyset
(e.g. when
\mathfrak{C}
is regular) and redundant otherwise.

The following result shows that when
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
,
(L,\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\cup\{\emptyset\})
is a topological space.

Proposition 21
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a closure operator over
L\subset\Lambda
. Then
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
iff
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\overline{{\mathfrak{C}}}
.

Proof 3.6
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
. As
\mathfrak{C}(s)=\bigcup_{P\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{P\})
,
\mathfrak{C}(s)=s
iff
s\in\overline{{\mathfrak{C}}}
. This shows that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
.
Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
.
Observe that for any
s\subseteq L
,
\mathfrak{C}(s)
is the smallest
\mathfrak{C}
-closed set containing
s
: if
s\subseteq t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, from
\mathfrak{C}(s)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(t)=t
it follows that
\mathfrak{C}(s)\subseteq t
. Now, since
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is closed by unions, for all
s\subseteq L
, if
s\neq\emptyset
, then
s^{\prime}=\bigcup_{P\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{P\})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and is the smallest
\mathfrak{C}
-closed set containing
s
: if
s\subseteq s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, then for all
P\in s
,
\mathfrak{C}(\{P\})\subseteq s^{\prime\prime}
, hence
s^{\prime}\subseteq s^{\prime\prime}
.
We deduce then
s^{\prime}=\bigcup_{P\in s}\mathfrak{C}(\{P\})=\mathfrak{C}(s)
, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
.

We present a useful application of
\mathsf{SU}
. We show that if a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L
is stable by union, then there exists a regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{D}
over
L
such that
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
.

Proposition 22
.

Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
be such that
L^{*}\subseteq L
and let
\mathfrak{C}
be a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L
satisfying
\mathsf{SU}
. Then the map

\mathfrak{C}^{*}:\wp(L)\to\wp(L)
given by
\mathfrak{C}^{*}(s)=\mathfrak{C}(s)\cup\mathfrak{C}(L^{*})
is a regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L
such that
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{C}^{*}
.

Proof 3.7
.

We first check that
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
is a closure operator. For all
s,t\subseteq L
, it is clear that
s\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{*}(s)
and that if
s\subseteq t
, then
\mathfrak{C}^{*}(s)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{*}(t)
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{C}^{*}(\mathfrak{C}^{*}(s))=\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{C}^{*}(s))\cup\mathfrak{C}(L^{*})=\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{C}(s)\cup\mathfrak{C}(L^{*}))\cup\mathfrak{C}(L^{*})=\mathfrak{C}(s)\cup\mathfrak{C}(L^{*})=\mathfrak{C}^{*}(s)
, by
\mathsf{SU}
and Proposition
21
.
That
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{C}^{*}
is an immediate consequence of the definition of
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
.

We now show that if
s,t
are
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
-closed, then
s\to_{L}t
is
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
-closed. First, for all
P\in L^{*}
, and
Q\in s
,
PQ\in L^{*}\subseteq t
. Hence
L^{*}\subseteq s\to_{L}t
. This implies that
\mathfrak{C}(L^{*})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(s\to_{L}t)
.
Now we have
\mathfrak{C}^{*}(s\to_{L}t)=\mathfrak{C}(s\to_{L}t)\cup\mathfrak{C}(L^{*})=\mathfrak{C}(s\to_{L}t)=s\to_{L}t
, where the last passage is a consequence of the fact that
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{C}^{*}
and Proposition
17
(which implies that
s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
).

3.1.3.
Soundness for adequate semantics

We show that, for any
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L\subseteq\Lambda
, any System
\mathsf{F}
type
\sigma
with
n
free variables can be interpreted as a function
|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
Then we introduce two conditions under which System
\mathsf{F}
typing judgements can be interpreted in the semantics generated by
\mathfrak{C}
. The first one (called condition
\mathsf{K}
) is a standard condition for realizability semantics (see
[
Kri93
]
). The second one (condition
\mathsf{R}
) is only required when
\mathfrak{C}
is defined over
L\subsetneq\Lambda
(e.g.
L=\mathcal{SN}
), and underlies a standard construction to prove soundness for Tait’s saturated sets semantics and Girard’s reducibility candidates semantics.
We prove that, if conditions
\mathsf{K}
and
\mathsf{R}
hold, then any judgement derivable in System
\mathsf{F}
has a sound interpretation in
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

{defi}

Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
and
\mathfrak{C}
be a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L
. For any type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
and
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}\subseteq\mathtt{TypeVar}
such that
\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}\cap\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)=\emptyset
, we define a map

|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
as follows

\begin{split}|X_{i}|^{\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{X}}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})&:=s_{i}\\
|\sigma\to\tau|^{\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{X}}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})&:=|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{X}}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\to_{L}|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{X}}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\\
|\forall X\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{X}}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})&:=\bigcap_{s\in S}|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C},\{X\}\cup\mathcal{X}}(s,s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\end{split}

(7)

We let
|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}=|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C},\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{card(\mathtt{FV}(\sigma))}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

A
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
is a map
\mathcal{M}:\mathtt{TypeVar}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
For any
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
\mathcal{M}
and type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, with
\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, we let

|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}=|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{M}(X_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{M}(X_{n}))
.

Finally, for all
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, with

\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, we let

\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}:=\bigcap_{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}}|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
.
A
\lambda
-term
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
will be called
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretable at
\sigma
.

Lemma 23
.

Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
be and
\mathfrak{C}
be a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L
. Then for all
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
\mathcal{M}
and type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,

\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof 3.8
.

From
\emptyset\subseteq s
it follows
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)\subseteq\mathfrak{C}(s)
, hence
\mathfrak{C}(\emptyset)
is a subset of every
\mathfrak{C}
-closed set.

From Lemma
23
and Proposition
18
we deduce the following property, which motivates the use of regular closure operators:

Lemma 24
.

Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
be and
\mathfrak{C}
be a regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L
. Then for all
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
\mathcal{M}
and type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,

L^{*}\subseteq|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

The following is a standard technical lemma in realizability semantics:

Lemma 25
.

For any
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L\subseteq\Lambda
, types
\sigma,\tau
, where

\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)\subseteq\{X,X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
and

\mathtt{FV}(\tau)\subseteq\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
,
|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}(|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{s}),\vec{s})=|\sigma[\tau/X]|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{s})
.

Proof 3.9
.

Induction on
\sigma
. We only prove the case
\sigma=\forall Y\sigma^{\prime}
. By induction hypothesis,
|\sigma^{\prime}|^{\mathfrak{C}}(|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{s}),s^{\prime},\vec{s})=|\sigma^{\prime}[\tau/X]|^{\mathfrak{C}}(s^{\prime},\vec{s})
. Since we can assume
Y
not free in
\tau
, we have
|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}(s^{\prime},\vec{s})=|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{s})
.

Now, for any term
P\in L
,

P\in|\sigma[\tau/X]|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{s})
iff for all
s^{\prime}\in S
,
P\in|\sigma^{\prime}[\tau/X]|^{\mathfrak{C}}(s^{\prime},\vec{s})
iff for all
s^{\prime}\in S
,

P\in|\sigma^{\prime}|^{\mathfrak{C}}(|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{s}),s^{\prime},\vec{s})
iff
P\in|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}(|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{s}),\vec{s})
.

We can now state the adequacy conditions required to interpret System
\mathsf{F}
type judgements.

{defi}

[adequacy]
Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
. A
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L\subseteq\Lambda
is
adequate
when it satisfies the conditions below:

(
\mathsf{K}
)

For all
s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and
M\in L
, if for all
P\in s
,
M[P/x]\in t
, then
\lambda x.M\in s\to_{L}t
;

(
\mathsf{R}
)

either
L=\Lambda
, or exists a regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{D}
over
L
such that
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is adequate and
L\subsetneq\Lambda
, by condition
\mathsf{R}
, there exists
\mathfrak{D}
regular such that
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
. By Proposition
18

\mathfrak{D}
generates a family of closed sets containing
L^{*}
. To prove that such a family is a semantics it suffices then to verify that
\mathfrak{D}
satisfies condition
\mathsf{K}
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
L\subseteq\Lambda
and
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{D}
be
\mathsf{F}
-closure operators over
L
. If
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies condition
\mathsf{K}
and
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
, then
\mathfrak{D}
satisfies condition
\mathsf{K}
.

Proof 3.10
.

As
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
, by Proposition
17
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Thus, if
s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{D}}
, then
s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, hence

s\to_{L}t\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
. This implies that if
M\in L
and for all
P\in s
,
M[P/x]\in t
, then
\lambda x.M\in s\to_{L}t
, since
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{K}
.

The following proposition allows one to show that a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator is adequate. We will exploit this result later in this section to prove that reducibility candidates are generated by an adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator.

Proposition 27
.

Let
L\subsetneq\Lambda
and
\mathfrak{C}
be a
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L
. If
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
and
\mathsf{K}
, then
\mathfrak{C}
is adequate.

Proof 3.11
.

By Proposition
22
, it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
satisfies condition
\mathsf{K}
, if
\mathfrak{C}
does. This follows from Lemma
26
.

We now prove that adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operators allows to interpret System
\mathsf{F}
judgements:

Theorem 28

(soundness)
.

For all
L\subseteq\Lambda
and adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L
the following holds: if
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
is derivable in System
\mathsf{F}
, where
\Gamma=\{x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\sigma_{n}\}
and
\mathtt{FV}(\Gamma)\cup\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)\subseteq\{X_{1},\dots,X_{p}\}
, then for any
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
\mathcal{M}
, for all
P_{1}\in|\sigma_{1}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}},\dots,P_{n}\in|\sigma_{n}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
,
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof 3.12
.

We argue by induction on a typing derivation of
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
.

•

If the derivation is
\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma
, then if
P\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
,
P=x[P/x]\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
.

•

If the derivation ends by

\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:\sigma\to\tau

, we can assume that
x
does not occur free in
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
and is different from
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
. By the induction hypothesis, for all
P\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
,
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n},P/x]\in|\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
and by the assumptions just made,
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n},P/x]=M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}][P/x]
.

Now, if
L=\Lambda
, then by
\mathsf{K}
and the fact that
M\in L
, we deduce

(\lambda x.M)[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\to|\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}=|\sigma\to\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
.
If
L\subsetneq\Lambda
, we argue as follows: since
\mathfrak{C}
is adequate, there exists a regular
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{D}
such that
\mathfrak{C}\leq\mathfrak{D}
. As
\mathfrak{D}
is regular, by Lemma
24
, for all
\mathfrak{D}
-interpretation
\mathcal{N}
, the sets
|\sigma_{i}|^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{N}}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
and
|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{N}}
contain
\mathtt{TermVar}
. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{D}
is adequate (by Lemma
26
), by the induction hypothesis, for all
P_{1}\in|\sigma_{1}|^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{N}}
,…,
P_{n}\in|\sigma_{n}|^{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{N}}
and
P\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{D}}
,
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n},P/x]\in|\tau|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{D}}
. By taking
P_{1}=x_{1},\dots,P_{n}=x_{n},P=x
, we deduce then
M\in|\tau|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{D}}\subseteq L
. Hence
M\in L
.
Now, since
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies condition
\mathsf{K}
and
M\in L
, we can conclude
(\lambda x.M)[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\to_{L}|\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}=|\sigma\to\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
.

•

If the derivation ends by

\Gamma\vdash M_{1}:\tau\to\sigma

\Gamma\vdash M_{2}:\tau

\Gamma\vdash M_{1}M_{2}:\sigma

, by the induction hypothesis
M_{1}[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\tau\to\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}=|\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\to_{L}|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
and
M_{2}[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
, hence
M_{1}[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]M_{2}[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]=M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
.

•

If the derivation ends by

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma

\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\sigma

, where
X\notin\mathtt{FV}(\Gamma)
, then for all
s\in S_{\mathfrak{C}}
and
i\leq n
,
|\sigma_{i}|^{\mathfrak{C}}(s,\mathcal{M}(X_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{M}(X_{p}))=|\sigma_{i}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, whence by the induction hypothesis we deduce
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}(s,\mathcal{M}(X_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{M}(X_{p}))
, and finally
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\forall X\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
.

•

If the derivation ends by

\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\sigma

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma[\tau/x]

, then by the induction hypothesis, for all
s\in S_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}(s,\mathcal{M}(X_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{M}(X_{p}))
, hence in particular
M[P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{n}/x_{n}]\in|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}(|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathcal{M}(X_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{M}(X_{n}))
. The claim follows then from Lemma
25
.

We conclude this subsection with some results which allow to compare a type
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
and its translation
\sigma^{+}\in\mathbf{\Pi}
and that will be used in the next sections.
We suppose given an adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator
\mathfrak{C}
over
L\subseteq\Lambda
.

Lemma 29
.

Let
s,u\subseteq L
,
I\neq\emptyset
and, for all
i\in I
,
t_{i}\subseteq L
.

(i)

s\to_{L}\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}=\bigcap_{i\in I}(s\to_{L}t_{i})
;

(ii)

(\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}\to_{L}s)\to_{L}u\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}((t_{i}\to_{L}s)\to_{L}u)
.

Proof 3.13
.

For (i), let
M\in s\to_{L}\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}
,
i\in I
and
P\in s
; then
MP\in t_{i}
, hence
M\in s\to_{L}t_{i}
for all
i\in I
and we conclude
M\in\bigcap_{i\in I}(s\to_{L}t_{i})
. Let now
M\in\bigcap_{i\in I}(s\to_{L}t_{i})
and
P\in s
; for all
i\in I
,
M\in s\to_{L}t_{i}
, hence
MP\in t_{i}
, and we conclude
MP\in\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}
, i.e.
M\in s\to_{L}\bigcap_{i}t_{i}
.

For (ii), first observe that for all
i\in I
,
t_{i}\to_{L}s\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}\to_{L}s
: if
P\in t_{i}\to_{L}s
and
Q\in\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}
, then, since
Q\in t_{i}
,
PQ\in s
. Let now
M\in(\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}\to_{L}s)\to_{L}u
,
i\in I
and
P\in t_{i}\to_{L}s
. Since
P\in\bigcap_{i\in I}t_{i}\to_{L}s
,
MP\in u
, and we conclude
M\in\bigcap_{i\in I}((u_{i}\to_{L}s)\to_{L}u)
.

Proposition 30
.

For any type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,

(i)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, then
\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq\|\sigma^{+}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}=\|\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
;

(ii)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, then
\|\sigma^{-}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Proof 3.14
.

We will prove that for any interpretation
\mathcal{M}:\mathtt{TypeVar}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\subseteq|\sigma^{+}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
and
|\sigma^{-}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
, if
\sigma\in\forall^{-}
, by induction on
\sigma
. If
\sigma=X
then
\sigma=\sigma^{+}=\sigma^{-}
and both claims are trivial. If
\sigma=\tau\to\rho\in\forall^{+}
, where
\tau=\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{p}\to Y\in\forall^{-}
and
\rho\in\forall^{+}
, then by induction hypothesis
|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\supseteq|\tau^{-}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
|\rho|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\rho^{+}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, hence
|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\tau^{-}\to\rho^{+}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
. Now, since
\tau^{-}\to\rho^{+}=(\forall\mathcal{X}_{1}\tau^{\prime}_{1}\to\dots\to\forall\mathcal{X}_{p}\tau^{\prime}_{p}\to Y)\to\forall\mathcal{X}\rho^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{+}=\forall\mathcal{X}_{1}\dots\forall\mathcal{X}_{p}\forall\mathcal{X}((\tau^{\prime}_{1}\to\dots\to\tau^{\prime}_{p}\to Y)\to\rho^{\prime})
, by Lemma
29
we get

|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\tau^{-}\to\rho^{+}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}=|\forall\mathcal{X}(\tau^{-}\to\rho^{\prime})|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\sigma^{+}|
.

If
\sigma=\tau\to\rho\in\forall^{-}
, where
\tau\in\forall^{+}
and
\rho\in\forall^{-}
then, by induction hypothesis,
|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\tau^{+}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
|\rho^{-}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\rho|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, and we conclude
|\sigma^{-}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Now, if
\sigma=\forall X\tau
and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, then
|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}=\bigcap_{s\in S}|\tau|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}[X\mapsto s]}\subseteq\bigcap_{s\in S}|\tau^{+}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}[X\mapsto s]}=|\sigma^{+}|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

It remains to prove
