Claim (i) follows from Theorem
71
and the fact that
(\_)^{\beta},(\_)^{\beta\eta},(\_)^{\beta sat}
are stable by substitution. Claim (ii) follows from claim (i) and
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime},\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}\leq(\_)^{\beta}\leq(\_)^{\beta\eta}
, which implies
\|\sigma\|^{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}},\|\sigma\|^{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\|\sigma\|^{\beta}

and
\|\sigma\|^{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}},\|\sigma\|^{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\|\sigma\|^{\beta\eta}
.

5.3.
Reynolds’ parametricity

Reynolds’ parametricity
[
Rey83
]
is a well-known approach to parametric polymorphism based on the technique of logical relations.
The general idea is that the interpretation of a universally quantified type
\forall X\sigma
should contain those polymorphic functions which behave uniformly in all possible instantiations. This is expressed by an invariance condition with respect to logical relations.
We shortly discuss why, in order to extend our approach to logical relations to Reynolds’ parametricity, one needs to enrich the structure of
\mathfrak{C}
-closed sets by equivalence relations extending the
\beta\eta
-theory (e.g. by considering
\mathsf{PER}s
, a standard approach to parametricity, see
[
BFSS90
]
).

As for logical relations semantics, in Reynolds’ semantics any type receives both an interpretation
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{0}
as a set or more generally as an object of some category, and an interpretation
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{1}
as a binary relation over
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{0}
. If one restricts to simple types, Reynolds’ parametricity coincides with invariance with respect to logical relations. Hence
71
shows that
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretable terms are Reynolds’ parametric in the case of simple types.

However, Reynolds’ semantics differs from the one defined in the previous pages for the interpretation of the universal quantifier: since the interpretation
\llbracket\forall X\sigma\rrbracket_{0}
of a universally quantified type must contain only those polymorphic functions which are “parametric in
X
”, the definition of
\llbracket\forall X\sigma\rrbracket_{0}
is given in terms of
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{1}
. In other words, while in the semantics of logical relations the two interpretations
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{0}
and
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{1}
are defined independently (Definitions
3.1.3
and
5.1.3
), this is not the case for Reynolds’ semantics. The dependency between the two notions of interpretation can be caracterised categorically (see
[
DR04
,
GFO15
,
GJF+15
]
) in terms of fibered functors and fibered natural transformations.

A fundamental ingredient of Reynolds’ parametricity is the validity of the so-called
Identity Extension Lemma
(
\mathsf{IEL}
)
[
Rey83
]
, which says that for any type
\sigma
, the relation obtained by instantiating the relational interpretation
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{1}
of
\sigma
on equality relations coincides with the equality relation over
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{0}
.

In our framework, a type
\sigma
with
n
free variables is interpreted as a map
|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
. For all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, let
\mathsf{Eq}_{s}
be the
\mathfrak{C}
-closure of the identity relation over
s
.
Given a
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
\mathcal{M}
, let
\mathsf{Eq}
be the
\mathfrak{C}
-closed relation assignment over
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}
such that, for all
X
,
\mathsf{Eq}(X)=\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathcal{M}(X)}
. Then
\mathsf{IEL}
can be formulated as the fact that, for any type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
,

\langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathsf{Eq}}\ =\ \mathsf{Eq}_{|\sigma|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{M}}}

(14)

In other words,
\mathsf{IEL}
expresses the fact that the maps over
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}
corresponding to types must be “equality-preserving”.
In more generality, in
[
GFO15
]
and
[
GJF+15
]
it is explained in detail how the interpretation of the quantifier in Reynolds’ parametric models can be defined in terms of equality-preserving functors.

The proposition below shows that
\mathsf{IEL}
fails in the case of

\beta\eta
-relations.

Proposition 73
.

Equation
14
fails for the
\beta\eta
-stable semantics.

Proof 5.15
.

Let
s
be any non-empty
\beta\eta
-stable set,
\mathcal{M}
be the interpretation given by
\mathcal{M}(X)=s
.
Let
M,N
be the two
\beta\eta
-distinct terms below:

\begin{split}M&:=\lambda f.\lambda x_{1}.\lambda x_{2}.x_{1}(fx_{2})\\
N&:=\lambda f.\lambda x_{1}.\lambda x_{2}.f(x_{1}x_{2})\end{split}

(15)

Let
\sigma=\forall Z(Z\to Z)\to(X\to Y)\to(X\to Y)
and
\mathcal{M}(X)=\Lambda
for all
X
.
While
(M,N)\notin\mathsf{Eq}_{|\sigma|^{\beta\eta}_{\mathcal{M}}}
, we claim that
M\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathsf{Eq}}\ N
.
Let
f
be any term variable and
R^{f}
be the
\beta\eta
-relation assignment over
\mathcal{N}
given by
R^{f}(X)=\{(P,Q)\mid fP\simeq_{\beta\eta}Q\}
, which is clearly
\beta\eta
-closed.
If
F\ \langle\forall Z(Z\to Z)\rangle^{\beta\eta}\ G
, then in particular

F\ \langle Z\to Z\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ G
. We claim that
for all
Q\in\Lambda
,
f(FQ)\simeq_{\beta\eta}G(fQ)
: we have
(Q,fQ)\in R^{f}(Z)
, so from
(FQ,G(fQ))\in R^{f}(Z)
we deduce the claim.
From
f(FQ)\simeq_{\beta\eta}G(fQ)
it follows then that for all
P,Q,P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
such that
P\simeq_{\beta\eta}P^{\prime}
,
Q\simeq_{\beta\eta}Q^{\prime}
,
MFPQ\simeq_{\beta\eta}NGP^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
, which concludes our proof.

The proof of Proposition
73
suggests that, in order to account Reyolds’ parametricity
in terms of sets of
\lambda
-terms, one must enrich the
\beta\eta
-equivalence relation in any closed set (e.g. by endowing the latter with a
\mathfrak{C}
-closed partial equivalence relation).

6.
Dinaturality

Dinaturality is an approach to parametric polymorphism in which types are interpreted as multivariant functors and terms as transformations between such functors satisfying a particular uniformity condition, which generalizes the usual definition of natural transformations.

In order to compare
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance and dinaturality, we introduce a syntactic formalisation of the dinaturality condition for pure
\lambda
-terms. We do this in two steps: first, we describe the dinaturality condition over the syntactic category generated by System
\mathsf{F}
as given by a family of
\beta\eta
-equations; this corresponds to the definition of syntactic dinaturality in
[
dL09
]
. Then, we provide a second syntactic dinaturality condition using indeterminates (i.e. free variables), which has the advantage of being expressed by a single equation, leading to a more manageable syntactic theory. While the first dinaturality condition easily follows from the second one, we do not know if the latter can be deduced from the former.

The main result of this section is that the closed terms which are
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant at a positive type are syntactically dinatural. We show two applications of this result: a second proof that closed simply typable terms are dinatural and a second completeness argument for positive types.

6.1.
Syntactic dinaturality

6.1.1.
Functorial polymorphism

We recall the interpretation of simple types as multivariant functors and simply typable terms as dinatural transformations.

{defi}

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and functors
F,G:(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{n}\times\mathcal{C}^{n}\to\mathcal{C}
, a
dinatural transformation
between
F
and
G
is a family of arrows
\theta_{\vec{A}}
satisfying the diagram below:

\textstyle{F\vec{A}\vec{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{\vec{A}}}
\textstyle{G\vec{A}\vec{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\vec{A}\vec{f}}
\textstyle{F\vec{B}\vec{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\vec{B}\vec{f}}
\scriptstyle{F\vec{f}\vec{A}}
\textstyle{G\vec{A}\vec{B}}
\textstyle{F\vec{B}\vec{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{\vec{B}}}
\textstyle{G\vec{B}\vec{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\vec{f}\vec{B}}

(16)

for any choice of objects
A_{i},B_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}
and arrows
f_{i}:A_{i}\to B_{i}
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a cartesian closed category,
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
, and
\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)\subseteq\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, then
\sigma
can be interpreted as a multivariant functor
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}:(\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathcal{C})^{n}\to\mathcal{C}
as follows:

\begin{split}\llbracket X_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}(\vec{A},\vec{B})\ &:=\ B_{i}\\
\llbracket\sigma\to\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}(\vec{A},\vec{B})\ &\ :=\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}(\vec{A},\vec{B})^{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}(\vec{B},\vec{A})}\end{split}

(17)

For any
\sigma,\tau\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
, a term
M
such that
x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
can be interpreted as a family of arrows
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\vec{A}}:\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\vec{A}\vec{A}\to\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\vec{A}\vec{A}
. In
[
GSS92
]
it is proved that such families are dinatural.

Theorem 74

(
[
GSS92
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be any cartesian closed category. If
x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
in
\lambda_{\to}
, then
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\vec{A}}:\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\vec{A}\vec{A}\to\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\vec{A}\vec{A}
is a dinatural transformation.

Remark 75
.

The extension of Theorem
74
to System
\mathsf{F}
is more delicate, and we won’t describe it here (the reader can look at
[
BFSS90
]
). There are two obstacles to extend the dinatural interpretation to second order quantifiers. First, the well-known fact that dinatural transformations do not generally compose; second, the fact that the definition of quantifiers requires the existence in the category
\mathcal{C}
of certain limits (called
ends
, see
[
Mac78
]
).

A dinatural interpretation of System
\mathsf{F}
is usually obtained by defining a restricted class of composable dinatural transformations over a suitable cartesian closed category
\mathcal{C}
in which “relativized” ends (see
[
BFSS90
]
) exist.

6.1.2.
Functorial polymorphism over the syntactic category

A first syntactic dinaturality condition is obtained by considering the syntactic category generated by typable terms, as we now recall.

{defi}

The
syntactic category

\mathcal{F}
has objects the types
\mathtt{T}
and arrows
\mathcal{F}[\sigma,\tau]
terms
M
with exactly one free variable
x
such that
x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
, considered up to
\beta\eta
-equivalence.

Any
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}
, with
\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)\subseteq\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, generates a functor

\widehat{\sigma}:(\mathcal{F}^{op}\times\mathcal{F})^{n}\to\mathcal{F}
defined as follows:

(1)

given types
\vec{\tau}
and
\vec{\rho}
,
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{\tau},\vec{\rho})
is the type obtained by replacing all negative occurrences of
X_{i}
by
\tau_{i}
and all positive occurrences of
X_{i}
by
\rho_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
;

(2)

given types
\vec{\tau},\vec{\tau}^{\prime},\vec{\rho},\vec{\rho}^{\prime}
and terms
terms
\vec{P}
,
\vec{Q}
, where
P_{i}\in\mathcal{F}(\tau_{i},\tau^{\prime}_{i})
and
Q_{i}\in\mathcal{F}(\rho_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{i})
, we define the terms
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{P},\vec{Q})\in\mathcal{F}(\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{\tau^{\prime}},\vec{\rho}),\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{\tau},\vec{\rho^{\prime}}))
by induction on
\sigma
as follows:

\begin{split}\widehat{X_{i}}(\vec{P},\vec{Q})\ &:=\ Q_{i}\\
\widehat{\tau\to\rho}(\vec{P},\vec{Q})\ &:=\ \left(\lambda x.\widehat{\tau}(\vec{Q},\vec{P})\Rightarrow\lambda x.\widehat{\rho}(\vec{P},\vec{Q})\right)x\\
\widehat{\forall X\tau}(\vec{P},\vec{Q})\ &:=\ \widehat{\tau}((\vec{P},x),(\vec{Q},x))\end{split}

(18)

Given types
\vec{\tau},\vec{\rho},\vec{\rho}^{\prime}
, and terms
\vec{P},\vec{Q}
, if
P_{i}=x
and
Q_{i}:\rho_{i}\to\rho^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, we let

\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{\tau},\vec{Q})
and
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{Q},\vec{\tau})
be shorthand for

\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{P},\vec{Q})
and
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{Q},\vec{P})
, respectively.

If
x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
holds in System
\mathsf{F}
, then
M
induces a (constant) family of arrows from
\widehat{\sigma}
to
\widehat{\tau}
: for all types
\vec{\rho}
,
x:\widehat{\sigma}\vec{\rho}\vec{\rho}\vdash M:\widehat{\tau}\vec{\rho}\vec{\rho}
(technically, this follows from Lemma
89
).
Since
M
corresponds to a constant family of arrows, the dinaturality condition for
M
in
\mathcal{F}
is expressed by a family of equations corresponding to diagram
16
:

\widehat{\tau}(\vec{\rho},\vec{P})\circ M\circ\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{P},\vec{\rho})\ \simeq_{\beta\eta}\ \widehat{\tau}(\vec{P},\vec{\tau})\circ M\circ\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{\tau},\vec{P})

(19)

for all types
\vec{\tau},\vec{\rho}
and arrows
\vec{P}
, where
P_{i}\in\mathcal{F}(\tau_{i},\rho_{i})
.

Let System
\mathsf{F}_{\times}
be the type system obtained from
F
by adding a unit type
\mathbf{1}
and binary products
\sigma\times\tau
. Its syntactic category
\mathcal{F}_{\times}
is cartesian closed
[
LS88
]
. Moreover, it is easily verified, that if
\mathcal{C}
is the syntactic category
\mathcal{F}
,
for all simple type
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
,
\widehat{\sigma}=\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{F}_{\times}}
.
From Theorem
74
, as remarked in
[
GSS92
]
, the following can then be deduced:

Proposition 76
.

All instances of equations
19
, for
\sigma,\tau\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
, hold in the theory generated by
\beta\eta
-equivalence.

Remark 77
.

An advantage of describing dinaturality in
\mathcal{F}
is that we can easily define the interpretation of all System
\mathsf{F}
types (see Remark
75
). It is natural to ask then whether Proposition
76
can be extended to all System
\mathsf{F}
types. In this section we will show that this can be done for positive types. However, syntactic dinaturality fails, as shown in detail in
[
dL09
]
, for types containing negative occurrences of quantifiers. We will provide a simple example at Remark
79
.

6.1.3.
Dinaturality with indeterminates

We now introduce a more uniform notion of syntactic dinaturality. The basic idea is to allow arrows in the syntactic category to have more than one variable. This corresponds, in the terminology of
[
LS88
]
, to considering the
polynomial
cartesian closed category
\mathcal{F}_{\times}[x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}},\dots,x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}]
generated by a finite number of indeterminates
x_{i}:\mathbf{1}\to\sigma_{i}
:

{defi}

Let
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\mathtt{T}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be distinct variables.
The
polynomial
category
\mathcal{F}_{\times}[x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}},\dots,x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}]
is obtained from

\mathcal{F}_{\times}
by adding the arrows
x_{i}:\mathbf{1}\to\sigma_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
.

In
[
LS88
]
it is proved that if a category
\mathcal{C}
is cartesian closed, then the polynomial category
\mathcal{C}[x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}},\dots,x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}]
constructed over it is cartesian closed too. We can thus conclude that
\mathcal{F}_{\times}[x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}},\dots,x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}]
is cartesian closed.

As
\mathcal{F}_{\times}[x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}},\dots,x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}]
is cartesian closed, Theorem
74
applies to it. This implies that, if
x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
, where
\sigma,\tau
are simple types, then all instances of equation
19
hold, where now the arrows
P_{i}
can contain occurrences of the indeterminates
x_{i}
.

If one considers distinct type variables
X_{i},Y_{i}
and indeterminates
f_{i}:\mathbf{1}\to(X_{i}\to Y_{i})
, all instances of equations
19
, which depend on the choice of types
\rho_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{i}
and arrows
P_{i}\in\mathcal{F}[\rho_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{i}]
, can be deduced, by substitution, from a single equation, by letting
\rho_{i}=X_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{i}=Y_{i}
and
P_{i}=f_{i}
. We now describe this idea in detail.

Let
(\mathtt{V}_{0},\mathtt{V}_{1})
be a partition of
\mathtt{TypeVar}
into two disjoint countable sets of type variables
\mathtt{V}_{0}=\{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},\dots\}
and
\mathtt{V}_{1}=\{Y_{0},Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots\}
. To avoid confusion, we will indicate generic variables in
\mathtt{TypeVar}
as
Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3},\dots
.

We consider a countable set of indeterminates
f_{1},f_{2},\dots
, where
f_{i}:\mathbf{1}\to(X_{i}\to Y_{i})
for all
i\geq 1
. For each type
\sigma
, we define terms
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma},\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}
coding the action of the functors
\widehat{\sigma}
.

We define
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma},\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}
by a simultaneous induction over
\sigma
:

\begin{split}\mathtt{H}_{Z_{i}}\ &:=\ \lambda x.f_{i}x\\
\mathtt{H}_{\tau\to\rho}\ &:=\ \left(\mathtt{K}_{\tau}\Rightarrow\mathtt{H}_{\rho}\right)\\
\mathtt{H}_{\forall Z_{i}\tau}\ &:=\ \mathtt{H}_{\tau}[\lambda x.x/f_{i}]\end{split}\qquad\qquad\begin{split}\mathtt{K}_{Z_{i}}\ &:=\ \lambda x.x\\
\mathtt{K}_{\tau\to\rho}\ &:=\ \left(\mathtt{H}_{\tau}\Rightarrow\mathtt{K}_{\rho}\right)\\
\mathtt{K}_{\forall Z_{i}\tau}\ &:=\ \mathtt{K}_{\tau}[\lambda x.x/f_{i}]\end{split}

(20)

For a simple type
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\dots\to\sigma_{n}\to Z_{i}
the definition of the terms
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}
and
\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}
can be unrolled as follows:

\begin{split}\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}\ =&\ \lambda x.\lambda x_{1}.\dots.\lambda x_{n}.f_{i}\big(x(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{1}}x_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{n}}x_{n})\big)\\
\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}\ =&\ \lambda x.\lambda x_{1}.\dots.\lambda x_{n}.x(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{1}}x_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{n}}x_{n})\end{split}

(21)

The following proposition shows that, for any type
\sigma
such that
\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)=\{Z_{i_{1}},\dots,Z_{i_{m}}\}
the terms
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma},\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}
code the functorial action of
\widehat{\sigma}
over the polynomial category
\mathcal{F}_{\times}[f_{i_{1}},\dots,f_{i_{n}}]
.

Proposition 78
.

For any type
\sigma
, with
\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)\subseteq\{Z_{i_{1}},\dots,Z_{i_{n}}\}
, types
\vec{\tau},\vec{\rho},\vec{\rho}^{\prime}
and terms
\vec{P}
, where
P_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{\times}(\rho_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{i})
,

\begin{matrix}\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{\tau},\vec{P})\ \simeq_{\beta}\ (\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}x)[\lambda x.P_{1}/f_{i_{1}},\dots,\lambda x.P_{n}/f_{i_{n}}]\\
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{P},\vec{\tau})\ \simeq_{\beta}\ (\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}x)[\lambda x.P_{1}/f_{i_{1}},\dots,\lambda x.P_{n}/f_{i_{n}}]\end{matrix}

(22)

Proof 6.1
.

Let
\vec{f}=(f_{1}x,\dots,f_{n}x)
and
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
By induction on
\sigma
, we prove that
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}x\simeq_{\beta}\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{x},\vec{f})
and

\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}x\simeq_{\beta}\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{f},\vec{x})
, from which the claim follows since

\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{\tau},\vec{P})\simeq_{\beta}\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{x},\vec{f})\theta
and

\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{P},\vec{\tau})\simeq_{\beta}\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{f},\vec{x})\theta
, where
\theta=[\lambda x.P_{1}/f_{i_{1}},\dots,\lambda x.P_{n}/f_{i_{n}}]
.

If
\sigma=X_{i}
, then
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{x},\vec{f})=f_{i}x\simeq_{\beta}\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}x
and

\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{f},\vec{x})=x=\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}x
.
If
\sigma=\tau\to\rho
, then observe that

\widehat{\tau\to\rho}(\vec{x},\vec{f})=\left(\lambda x.\widehat{\tau}(\vec{f},\vec{x})\Rightarrow\lambda x.\widehat{\rho}(\vec{x},\vec{f})\right)x\simeq_{\beta}\lambda y.\left(\widehat{\rho}(\vec{x},\vec{f})\left[x\left(\widehat{\tau}(\vec{f},\vec{x})[y/x]\right)/x\right]\right)

(23)

Now

\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}x\simeq_{\beta}(\mathtt{K}_{\tau}\Rightarrow\mathtt{H}_{\rho})x\simeq_{\beta}\lambda y.\mathtt{H}_{\rho}(x(\mathtt{K}_{\tau}y))\simeq_{\beta}\lambda y.(\widehat{\rho}(\vec{x},\vec{f}))\left[x(\widehat{\tau}(\vec{f},\vec{x})[y/x])/x\right]\simeq_{\beta}\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{x},\vec{f})
, where in the last step we used the induction hypothesis, which implies that

\widehat{\tau}(\vec{f},\vec{x})\simeq_{\beta}\mathtt{K}_{\tau}x
and
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{x},\vec{f})\simeq_{\beta}\mathtt{H}_{\rho}
.
One can argue similarly for
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{f},\vec{x})
.
Finally, if
\sigma=\forall X_{i}\tau
, then

\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{x},\vec{f})=\widehat{\tau}((\vec{x},x_{i}),(\vec{f},x_{i}))\simeq_{\beta}\mathtt{H}_{\tau}[\lambda x.x/f_{i}]\simeq_{\beta}\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}
, by using the induction hypothesis and the fact that
\widehat{\tau}((\vec{x},x_{i})(\vec{f},x_{i}))\simeq_{\beta}\widehat{\tau}(\vec{x},\vec{f})[\lambda x.x/f_{i}]
.
One can argue similarly for
\widehat{\sigma}(\vec{f},\vec{x})
.

The dinaturality condition for a term
M
such that
x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau
can now be expressed by the commutation of a single diagram:

\textstyle{\widehat{\sigma}\vec{X}\vec{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}
\textstyle{\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{H}_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\sigma}\vec{Y}\vec{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{Y}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\sigma}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}
\textstyle{\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{K}_{\tau}}

(24)

i.e. by a single equation

\mathtt{H}_{\tau}\circ M\circ\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ \mathtt{K}_{\tau}\circ M\circ\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}

(25)

where
\gamma
is either
\beta
or
\beta\eta
,
which can be reformulated as

\mathtt{H}_{\sigma\to\tau}M\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ \mathtt{K}_{\sigma\to\tau}M

(26)

This leads to the following definition:

{defi}

[
\beta
and
\beta\eta
-dinaturality]
Let
\sigma
be a type,
\gamma
be either
\beta
or
\beta\eta
and
M
be a
\lambda
-term. Then
M
is
\gamma
-dinatural at
\sigma
if
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}M\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ \mathtt{K}_{\sigma}M
.

{exa}

Let
\sigma
be the type
\forall Z_{i}((Z_{i}\to Z_{i})\to(Z_{i}\to Z_{i}))
. The
\beta
-dinaturality condition associated with a closed
\lambda
-term
M
and
\sigma
is the equation

f_{i}\big(M\ \lambda y.x_{1}(f_{i}y)\ x_{2}\big)\ \simeq_{\beta}\ M\ \lambda y.f_{i}(x_{1}y)\ f_{i}x_{2}

(27)

which can be illustrated by the diagram below:

\textstyle{X_{i}\to X_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}
\textstyle{X_{i}\to X_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{H}_{Z_{i}\to Z_{i}}}
\textstyle{Y_{i}\to X_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{K}_{Z_{i}\to Z_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{H}_{Z_{i}\to Z_{i}}}
\textstyle{X_{i}\to Y_{i}}
\textstyle{Y_{i}\to Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}
\textstyle{Y_{i}\to Y_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{K}_{Z_{i}\to Z_{i}}}

(28)

If
M
is a closed term of type
\sigma
, the
\beta\eta
-normal form of
M
is of the form
\lambda y.\lambda x.(y)^{n}x
, for some
n\geq 0
. Then we can check that Equation
27
reduces to the valid equation

f_{i}\big(\underbrace{x_{1}(f_{i}\dots x_{1}(f_{i}}_{n\text{ times}}x_{2})\dots)\big)\ \simeq_{\beta}\ \underbrace{f_{i}(x_{1}\dots f_{i}(x_{1}}_{n\text{ times}}(f_{i}x_{2}))\dots)

(29)

We can conclude then that
M
is
\beta
-dinatural at
\sigma
.

Remark 79
.

Dinaturality fails for non positive types. Let
M
be the term
\lambda x.\lambda y.xy
and
\sigma=(\forall Y(Y\to Y))\to(X\to X)
. Although
\vdash M:\sigma
holds,
M
is not
\beta
-dinatural nor
\beta\eta
-dinatural at
\sigma
, since

\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}M\ \simeq_{\beta}\ \lambda x_{1}.\lambda x_{2}.f(x_{1}x_{2})\ \not\simeq_{\beta\eta}\ \lambda x_{1}.\lambda x_{2}.x_{1}(fx_{2})\ \simeq_{\beta}\ \mathtt{K}_{\sigma}M

(30)

The investigation of syntactic dinaturality for non positive types requires then to consider equational theories which extend
\beta\eta
-equivalence.
Equation
30
was already considered in Subsection
5.3
as a consequence of the
\mathsf{IEL}
for Reynolds’ parametricity. In fact, all equations arising from dinaturality can be deduced from an axiomatization of Reynolds’ parametricity, as shown in
[
PA93
]
.

6.2.
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance implies dinaturality

We prove that, when
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
, the closed terms which are
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant at
\sigma
are also
\beta
and
\beta\eta
-dinatural at
\sigma
. This result generalizes a result contained in
[
Pis17
]
, which was limited to the
\beta\eta
-stable semantics and to simple types.

We first show that, similarly to the case of completeness,
\gamma
-dinaturality at a
\forall^{+}
type (for
\gamma=\beta,\beta\eta
) can be deduced from
\gamma
-dinaturality at
\mathbf{\Pi}
types, by means of the following:

Lemma 80
.

For all
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
and term
M
, if
M
is
\gamma
-dinatural at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
, then
M
is
\gamma
-dinatural at
\sigma
, where
\gamma
is either
\beta
or
\beta\eta
.

Proof 6.2
.

Since
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}=\mathtt{H}_{\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)}[\lambda x.x/f_{i_{1}},\dots,\lambda x.x/f_{i_{p}}]
and

\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}=\mathtt{K}_{\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)}[\lambda x.x/f_{i_{1}},\dots,\lambda x.x/f_{i_{p}}]
, where

\mathtt{BV}(\sigma)=\{Z_{i_{1}},\dots,Z_{i_{p}}\}
, if
M
satisfies the dinaturality condition at
\sigma
, then, by substitution,
M
satisfies the dinaturality condition at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
.

Let now
\gamma
be either
\beta
or
\beta\eta
and
\mathfrak{C}
be an adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L\subseteq\Lambda
such that
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\gamma}
.
We introduce two models and a relation assignment which corresponds to
\gamma
-dinaturality.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}:\mathtt{TypeVar}\to S
be given, for any
Z_{i}
, by
\mathcal{M}_{1}(Z_{i})=\Lambda
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}(Z_{i})=f_{i}\Lambda
, where
f_{i}\Lambda
is the
\gamma
-closure of the set of all
\lambda
-terms of the form
f_{i}Q
, where
Q\in\Lambda
. Let moreover
R^{f}
be the
\gamma
-closed (hence
\mathfrak{C}
-closed) relation assignment over
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}
such that, for any
Z_{i}
,
P\in\Lambda
and
Q\in f_{i}\Lambda
,
(P,Q)\in R^{f}(Z_{i})
iff
f_{i}P\simeq_{\gamma}Q
.

Finally, let
\mathcal{N}:\mathtt{V}_{1}\cup\mathtt{V}_{2}\to S
be the interpretation such that, for all
u\geq 0
,
\mathcal{N}(X_{i})=\mathcal{M}_{1}(Z_{i})
and
\mathcal{N}(Y_{i})=\mathcal{M}_{2}(Z_{i})
.

Proposition 81
.

For any type
\tau
,

\begin{matrix}\mathtt{H}_{\tau}\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{X}\to\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}&\mathtt{K}_{\tau}\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}\to\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\\
\mathtt{K}_{\tau}\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{X}\to\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{X}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}&\mathtt{H}_{\tau}\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{Y}\to\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\end{matrix}

(31)

Proof 6.3
.

We argue by induction on
\tau
: if
\tau=Z_{i}
, then
\mathtt{H}_{\tau}=\lambda x.f_{i}x\in|X_{i}\to Y_{i}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\mathtt{K}_{\tau}=\lambda x.x\in|X_{i}\to X_{i}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}},|Y_{i}\to Y_{i}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
.
If
\tau=\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{m}\to Z_{i}
for some
m\geq 1
, then let
E\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{X}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}},F\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{X}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}},G\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
and
E_{i}\in|\widehat{\tau}_{i}\vec{Y}\vec{X}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}},F_{i}\in|\widehat{\tau}_{i}\vec{X}\vec{X}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}},G_{i}\in|\widehat{\tau}_{i}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
, for
1\leq i\leq m
. Then, by induction hypothesis we have

\begin{matrix}\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{i}}E_{i}\in|\widehat{\tau}_{i}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}&\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{i}}G_{i}\in|\widehat{\tau}_{i}\vec{X}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\\
\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{i}}E_{i}\in|\widehat{\tau}_{i}\vec{X}\vec{X}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}&\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{i}}F_{i}\in|\widehat{\tau}_{i}\vec{X}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\end{matrix}

(32)

from which we obtain

\begin{split}\mathtt{H}_{\tau}EG_{1}\dots G_{n}\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ &f_{i}\big(E(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{1}}G_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{n}}G_{n})\big)\in|Y|^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathcal{N}}\\
\mathtt{H}_{\tau}FE_{1}\dots E_{n}\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ &f_{i}\big(F(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{1}}E_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{n}}E_{n})\big)\in|Y|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\\
\mathtt{K}_{\tau}EF_{1}\dots F_{n}\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ &E(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{1}}F_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{n}}F_{n})\in|X|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\\
\mathtt{K}_{\tau}GE_{1}\dots E_{n}\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ &G(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{1}}E_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{n}}E_{n})\in|Y|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}\end{split}

(33)

where
\gamma
is either
\beta
or
\beta\eta
, by exploiting the fact that
\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{X}=\widehat{\tau}_{1}\vec{X}\vec{Y}\to\dots\to\widehat{\tau}_{n}\vec{X}\vec{Y}\to X_{i}
.

For any term variable
x
,
\mathtt{H}_{\tau}x\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{Y}\vec{Y}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}=|\tau|_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\mathtt{K}_{\tau}x\in|\widehat{\tau}\vec{X}\vec{X}|_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{C}}=|\tau|_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{C}}
. Hence we can ask whether
\mathtt{H}_{\tau}x
and
\mathtt{K}_{\tau}x
are related under the
\mathfrak{C}
-closed relation assignment
R^{f}
. This is shown by the proposition below in the case of simple types:

Proposition 82
.

Let
\gamma
be either
\beta
or
\beta\eta
.
For all
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
and variable
x
,

(i)

(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}x)\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ (\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}x)
;

(ii)

if
P\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}_{1}},Q\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}
and
P\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ Q
, then
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}P\simeq_{\gamma}\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}Q
.

Proof 6.4
.

We prove both claims simultaneously by induction on
\sigma
. If
\sigma=Z_{i}
then
\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}x\simeq_{\gamma}x,\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}x\simeq_{\gamma}f_{i}x
and
x\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ f_{i}x
, from which we deduce
\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}x\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ \mathtt{H}_{\sigma}x
; moreover, if
P\ \langle Z_{i}\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ Q
, then
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}P\simeq_{\gamma}f_{i}P\simeq_{\gamma}Q\simeq_{\gamma}\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}Q
.

If
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\to\dots\to\sigma_{n}\to Z_{i}
for some
n\geq 1
, then suppose
P_{i}\in|\sigma_{i}|_{\mathcal{M}_{1}},Q_{i}\in|\sigma_{i}|_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}
and
P_{i}\ \langle\sigma_{i}\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ Q_{i}
, for all
1\leq i\leq n
; then

P:=\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}xP_{1}\dots P_{n}\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ x(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{1}}P_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{n}}P_{n})

(34)

is related to

Q:=\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}xQ_{1}\dots Q_{n}\ \simeq_{\gamma}\ f_{i}\Big(x(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{1}}Q_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{n}}Q_{n})\Big)

(35)

Indeed, by induction hypothesis,
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{i}}P_{i}\simeq_{\gamma}\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{i}}Q_{i}
, hence
x(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{1}}P_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{n}}P_{n})\simeq_{\gamma}

x(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{1}}Q_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{n}}Q_{n})
so
f_{i}P\simeq_{\gamma}Q
.

Suppose now
P\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}_{1}},Q\in|\sigma|_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}
and
P\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ Q
, then let

P^{\prime}:=\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}Px_{1}\dots x_{n}\simeq_{\beta}f_{i}\Big(P(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{1}}x_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{n}}x_{n})\Big)

(36)

and

Q^{\prime}:=\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}Qx_{1}\dots x_{n}\simeq_{\beta}Q(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{1}}x_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{n}}x_{n})

(37)

By induction hypothesis
(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{i}}x_{i})\ \langle\sigma_{i}\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ (\mathtt{H}_{\sigma_{i}}x_{i})
hence, by hypothesis
P(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{1}}x_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\sigma_{n}}x_{n})
is related to
Q^{\prime}
, that is
P^{\prime}\simeq_{\gamma}Q^{\prime}
. We conclude that
\mathtt{H}_{\sigma}P\simeq_{\gamma}\mathtt{K}_{\sigma}Q
.

We can now state and prove that parametric terms are
\beta\eta
-dinatural (resp.
\beta
-dinatural) for
\forall^{+}
(resp.
\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
) types:

Theorem 83

(
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance implies dinaturality)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator,
M\in\Lambda
and
\sigma
a type

(i)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
,
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\beta\eta}
, and
M
is
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant at
\sigma
, then
M
is
\beta\eta
-dinatural at
\sigma
;

(ii)

if
\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
,
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\beta}
, and
M
is
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant at
\sigma
, then
M
is
\beta
-dinatural at
\sigma
.

Proof 6.5
.

We only prove claim (ii). Let
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
and
M
be
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant at
\sigma
. By Proposition
65
,
M
is
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
.
Let
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)=\tau_{1}\to\dots\to\tau_{n}\to Z_{i}
, for some
n\geq 0
and
let
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
R^{f}
be as in Definition
6.2
. By Propositions
81
and
82
,
\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{i}}g_{i}\in|\tau_{i}|_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}
,
\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{i}}g_{i}\in|\tau_{i}|_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}
and
(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{i}}g_{i})\ \langle\tau_{i}\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ (\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{i}}g_{i})
. As
M
is
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant,
M\ \langle\tau\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ M
, hence for some
P\simeq_{\beta}M(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{1}}g_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{n}}g_{n})
and

Q\simeq_{\beta}M(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{1}}g_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{n}}g_{n})
,
P\ \langle Z_{i}\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R^{f}}\ Q
, that is

f_{i}\big(M(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{1}}g_{1})\dots(\mathtt{K}_{\tau_{n}}g_{n})\big)\ \simeq_{\beta}\ M(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{1}}g_{1})\dots(\mathtt{H}_{\tau_{n}}g_{n})
, whence
M
is
\beta
-dinatural at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
, hence
\beta
-dinatural at
\sigma
by Lemma
80
.

Remark 84
.

Theorem
83
can be compared with the well-known fact that Reynolds’ parametricity implies dinaturality, or more precisely, that all instances of the dinaturality equations
19
follow from a “parametricity axiom”, i.e. an axiom stating that any term of a given type
\sigma
is Reynolds’ parametric at
\sigma

[
PA93
]
.

As we discussed in Subsection
5.3
, Reynold’s parametricity coincides with invariance with respect to logical relations only for simple types. Moreover, it was seen that Reynolds’ parametricity implies the validity of some equations which extend
\beta\eta
-equivalence, as a consequence of
\mathsf{IEL}
. For instance, all equations
19
can be deduced from parametricity.
On the contrary, since
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance does not induce new equations between realizers, it
need not imply dinaturality for types containing negative occurrences of quantifiers (see Remark
79
).

6.3.
Two applications of syntactic dinaturality

We present two applications of Theorem
83
. First, we extend Theorem
74
, which states that closed simply typed
\lambda
-terms are dinatural to the cases of
\beta
and
\beta\eta
-dinaturality and to
\forall^{+}
types.
Then we show a second completeness argument based on a result in
[
Pis17
]
which shows that
\beta\eta
-dinaturality implies typability for closed normal
\lambda
-terms.

By considering
(\_)^{\beta}
or
(\_)^{\beta\eta}
as
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator and composing Theorem
83
with the Theorem
68
, we obtain the following:

Theorem 85
.

Let
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
and
M
be a closed
\lambda
-term. If
\vdash M:\sigma
, then
M
is
\beta
-dinatural at
\sigma
.

This theorem can be compared with Theorem
74
. First, in Theorem
85

\beta\eta
-dinaturality is replaced by
\beta
-dinaturality, so it can be seen as a slight refinement of Theorem
74
. Moreover, while Theorem
74
is proved by a categorial argument,
Theorem
85
exploits syntactic dinaturality and the
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance theorem.

We now deduce a second completeness argument from Theorem
83
. We exploit the following result from
[
Pis17
]
:

Theorem 86

(dinaturality implies typability)
.

If
\sigma\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
and
M
is a closed
\beta
-normal (resp.
\beta\eta
-normal) term
\beta
-dinatural at
\sigma
(resp.
\beta\eta
-dinatural at
\sigma
), then
\vdash M:\sigma
.

The argument in
[
Pis17
]
is proved only for
\beta\eta
-dinaturality, but the adaptation to
\beta
-dinaturality is straightforward.
By composing Theorem
71
and Theorem
83
, we get the following completeness results:

Theorem 87

(completeness by dinaturality)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator over
L\subseteq\Lambda
satisfying
\mathsf{SU}
,
\mathsf{SA}
and
\mathsf{SS}
.

(i)

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\beta\eta}
. Let
M
be a closed
\lambda
-term and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
. If
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
, then there exists
M^{\prime}\simeq_{\beta\eta}M
such that
\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma
.

(ii)

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\beta}
. Let
M
be a closed
\lambda
-term and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
. If
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
, then there exists
M^{\prime}\simeq_{\beta}M
such that
\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma
.

Proof 6.6
.

We only prove claim (i), the second being proved similarly.
From
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\beta\eta}
and
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
we deduce
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\beta\eta}
. By Proposition
30
we deduce then
M\in\|\sigma^{+}\|^{\beta\eta}\subseteq\|\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)\|^{\beta\eta}
. Hence, by Theorem
71
,
M
is
\beta\eta
-invariant at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
, and by Theorem
83
,
M
is
\beta\eta
-dinatural at
\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
. By Theorem
86
we have then
\vdash M^{\prime}:\mathsf{sk}(\sigma)
, for some
M^{\prime}\simeq_{\beta\eta}M
, and we conclude
\vdash M^{\prime\prime}:\sigma
, for some
M^{\prime\prime}\simeq_{\beta\eta}M^{\prime}\simeq_{\beta\eta}M
by Theorem
14
..

Theorem
87
is slighty weaker than Corollary
54
. In particular, it depends on the hypotheses of Theorem
71
(i.e. that
\mathfrak{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU},\mathsf{SA}
and
\mathsf{SS}
).

7.
Conclusions and open questions

7.1.
Equivalence of realizability and parametricity for positive types

By putting together Theorem
14
, Theorem
71
, Theorem
83
and Theorem
86

we deduce that, in the case of positive types, realizability, invariance with respect to logical relations, dinaturality and typability are equivalent properties for closed normal
\lambda
-terms:

Theorem 88
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an adequate
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator satisfying
\mathsf{SU}
,
\mathsf{SA}
and
\mathsf{SS}
.

(i)

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\beta\eta}
. Let
M
be a closed
\beta\eta
-normal
\lambda
-term and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
. Then the following are equivalent:

•

M
is
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretable at
\sigma
;

•

M
is
\beta\eta
-invariant at
\sigma
;

•

M
is
\beta\eta
-dinatural at
\sigma
;

•

for some
M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\to_{\eta}^{*}M
,
\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma
.

(ii)

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}\leq(\_)^{\beta}
. Let
M
be a closed
\beta
-normal
\lambda
-term and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}
. Then the following are equivalent:

•

M
is
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretable at
\sigma
;

•

M
is
\beta
-invariant at
\sigma
;

•

M
is
\beta
-dinatural at
\sigma
;

•

\vdash M:\sigma
.

It seems reasonable to expect that completeness cannot be extended beyond the classes
\forall^{+}_{\mathtt{P}}/\forall^{+}
. For instance, by taking the semantics
\mathcal{S}_{\beta}
, it can be seen that completeness fails for
\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}
types as follows: by a result in
[
GLT89
]
there exists a total unary recursive function
f
such that, for no
\lambda
-term
M_{f}
computing
f
,
M_{f}
can be given the
\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}
type
\mathbf{Nat}\to\mathbf{Nat}
(or, equivalently, the
\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}
type
\sigma=\forall X(\mathbf{Nat}\to(X\to X)\to(X\to X))
), where
\mathbf{Nat}=\forall X((X\to X)\to(X\to X))
. By letting
M_{f}
be any closed
\beta
-normal
\lambda
-term which represents
{f}
(see
[
Bar76
]
or
[
BGP94
]
for the construction of
M_{f}
), it can be verified that
M_{f}\in\|\mathbf{Nat}\to\mathbf{Nat}\|^{{\beta}}=\|\sigma\|^{{\beta}}
(where the last equality is a consequence of Lemma
29
). Hence there exists a closed
\beta
-normal realizer of a
\mathsf{co}\text{-}\mathbf{\Pi}
type
\sigma
such that
\vdash M:\sigma
is not derivable in System
\mathsf{F}
.

Theorem
88
shows that the parametricity condition expressed by realizability, invariance and dinaturality coincide for positive types. As observed in Subsection
5.3
and in Remark
77
, in order to compare realizability with parametricity for types containing negative occurrences of the universal quantifier, one needs to consider a more extensional approach to realizability, including equations between realizers extending
\beta\eta
-equivalence.

7.2.
Open problems and future work

As discussed in Subsection
3.2
, the completeness argument as well as the approach to logical relations here developed do not apply to regular closure operators, hence to Girard’s original reducibility candidates. We
agree with Colin Riba’s remark on the “not trivial and somehow mysterious” -
[
Rib07
]
, p.2 - structure of reducibility candidates.
A possible approach might be to consider, instead of the infinite context
\Gamma^{\infty}
, contexts of the form
\Gamma_{M}=\{x_{1}:\forall XX,\dots,x_{n}:\forall XX\}
, where
\mathtt{FV}(M)=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
. For any type
\sigma
, the set
\sigma^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}}}
obtained by
\beta
-closure of the set
\{M\mid\Gamma_{M}\vdash M:\sigma\}
, is a reducibility candidate. Indeed, for any neutral and normal term
M
and any type
\sigma
, it can be derived that
\Gamma_{M}\vdash M:\sigma
, so
\sigma^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}}}
is closed with respect to condition
\mathsf{CR}3
.

Our treatment of logical relations within realizability semantics uses stability by union in an essential way. Appeal to this property significantly simplifies the structure of the semantics, and was justified by the fact that all semantics considered are stable by union. However, as shown in
[
Rib07
]
, this property is related to a standardization result for the reduction relations employed (the “principal reduct property” recalled in Appendix
B
), which might fail for reduction relations and type systems different from those here considered.

Finally, a natural extension of the approach here presented is by considering Krivine’s second order functional arithmetic
\mathcal{AF}2

[
Kri93
]
, which extends System
\mathsf{F}
by means of first-order terms and
n
-ary predicates. Realizability semantics straightforwardly extends to
\mathcal{AF}2
, yielding completeness results analogous to those which hold for System
\mathsf{F}
(see
[
FN98
]
).
The realizability semantics of
\mathcal{AF}2
seems a good candidate to relate the results here presented with those in
[
Wad07
]
and
[
BL11
]

on realizability and parametricity treated as formal translations in the second order predicate calculus.
As recalled in the introduction, these results show that
n
-ary parametricity composed with realizability corresponds to
n+1
-ary parametricity.
This approach highlights the formal similarity between the clauses defining realizability and those defining parametricity. This similarity is implicit in our formalization, as it appears by inspecting the treatment of adequate semantics and the soundness theorem on the one side (Section
3
) and of logical relations and its soundness theorem on the other side (Section
5
). In
\mathcal{AF}2
one might try to make this explicit as follows. For any System
\mathsf{F}
type
\sigma
we can define a predicate
x\in\boldsymbol{\sigma}
expressing the property “
x
is a realizer of
\sigma
”. We can then define a “realizability translation” consisting in proving that
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
implies
M\in\|M\in\boldsymbol{\sigma}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
for
M
closed. For instance, we have
\mathbf{X\to X}=\forall u(u\in\mathbf{X}\to xu\in\mathbf{X})
and for
I_{X}=\lambda x.x\in\|X\to X\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
, we can immediately verify that
I_{X}\in\|I_{X}\in\mathbf{X\to X}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
holds
2
2

2

Where a
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
\mathcal{M}
for
\mathcal{AF}2
associates term variables
x,y
with
\beta\eta
-equivalence classes of
\lambda
-terms
x_{\mathcal{M}},y_{\mathcal{M}}
.
.

The generalization of the statement of Theorem
71
to
\mathcal{AF}2
might be as follows: for any adequate (and
\mathsf{SU}
, etc.)
\mathsf{F}
-closure operator and closed
\lambda
-terms
P,Q
,
P\in\|Q\in\boldsymbol{\sigma}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
iff for all
\mathfrak{C}
-interpretation
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathfrak{C}
-relation assignment
R
over
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}
,
P\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R}\ Q
.
On the one hand, this result would generalize Theorem
71
showing that closed terms
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
are
\mathfrak{C}
-invariant (passing through
M\in\|M\in\boldsymbol{\sigma}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
). On the other hand, it would show that realizability increases the arity of logical relations, as the realizability translation
M\in\|\sigma\|^{\mathfrak{C}}\ \mapsto\ M\in\|M\in\boldsymbol{\sigma}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
would turn out equivalent to the
\mathfrak{C}
-invariance condition
M\ \langle\sigma\rangle^{\mathfrak{C}}_{R}\ M
for closed terms.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
8

Before proving Proposition
8
, we establish some useful lemmas.

Lemma 89
.

For all derivation
D
of
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
and for any type
\rho
, by replacing any occurrence of
X
by
\rho
in
D
one obtains a derivation
D[\rho/X]
of
\Gamma[\rho/X]\vdash M:\sigma[\rho/X]
.

Proof A.1
.

First observe that we can suppose that the variables appearing bound in
D
are not among the free variables of
\rho
. Then we can argue by induction on
D
. We only consider the two interesting cases below:

•

if
D
has conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\forall Y\tau
and ends by a
\forall
I rule whose immediate sub-derivation
D^{\prime}
has conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau
, then by induction hypothesis
D^{\prime}[\rho/X]
has conclusion
\Gamma[\rho/X]\vdash M:\tau[\rho/X]
and by the assumption made,
Y
does not occur free in
\Gamma[\rho/X]
. Hence by the
\forall
I rule, we deduce
\Gamma[\rho/X]\vdash M:\forall Y(\tau[\rho/X])
. The derivation obtained is of the form
D[\rho/X]
and we conclude by observing that

\forall Y(\tau[\rho/X])=(\forall Y\tau)[\rho/X]
as
Y
does not occur free in
\rho
.

•

if
D
has conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau[\rho^{\prime}/X]
and ends by a
\forall
E rule whose immediate sub-derivation
D^{\prime}
has conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\forall Y\tau
, then by induction hypothesis
D^{\prime}[\rho/X]
has conclusion
\Gamma[\rho/X]\vdash M:(\forall Y\tau)[\rho/X]
. By the assumption made
(\forall Y\tau)[\rho/X]=\forall Y(\tau[\rho/X])
, hence by the
\forall
E rule we deduce
\Gamma[\rho/X]\vdash M:\tau[\rho/X][\rho^{\prime\prime}/Y]
, where
\rho^{\prime\prime}=\rho^{\prime}[\rho/X]
. Now we can conclude since
\tau[\rho/X][\rho^{\prime\prime}/Y]=\tau[\rho^{\prime}/Y][\rho/X]
.

For any derivation
D
of a type judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
, we let
r(D)
indicate the number of rules occurring in
D
. We call
D

\forall
E-free
when it contains no occurrence of the
\forall
E rule.

Lemma 90
.

Suppose
M
is
\beta
-normal,
\Gamma\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
. If
D
is a
\forall
E-free derivation of
\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\sigma
, then
D
ends by a
\forall
I rule.

Proof A.2
.

If
D
is
\forall
E-free and does not end by a
\forall
I rule, then either
M=x
and
x:\forall X\sigma\in\Gamma
or
D
must end by a
\to
E rule. The first case can be excluded since
\forall X\sigma\notin\forall^{-}
. Hence, since
M
is
\beta
-normal,
M=xM_{1}\dots M_{p}
, for some
p\geq 1
, where
\Gamma
contains a type declaration of the form
x:\sigma_{1}\to\dots\to\sigma_{p}\to\forall X\sigma
and
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:\sigma_{i}
is derivable for
i=1,\dots,p
.
But this is also impossible since
\sigma_{1}\to\dots\to\sigma_{p}\to\forall X\sigma\notin\forall^{-}
.

The following two lemmas are easily established by induction, respectively, on derivations and on types.

Lemma 91
.

Suppose
M
is
\beta
-normal and
D
is a
\forall
E-free derivation of
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
. Then, for all variable
X\in\mathtt{FV}(\Gamma)\cup\mathtt{FV}(\sigma)
, if
X
only occurs in positive (resp. negative) positions in
\sigma
and in negative (resp. positive) positions in the types in
\Gamma
, for all judgement
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime}
in
D
,
X
only occurs in negative (resp. positive) positions in the types in
\Gamma^{\prime}
and only occurs in positive (resp. negative) positions in
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma 92
.

\sigma[\tau/X]\in\forall^{+}
iff
\sigma\in\forall^{+}
and one of the following holds:

•

X
only occurs in positive positions in
\sigma
and
\tau\in\forall^{+}
;

•

X
only occurs in negative positions in
\sigma
and
\tau\in\forall^{-}
;

•

\tau\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
.

Proof A.3

(Proof of Proposition
8
)
.

We argue by induction on the construction of
D
. The only non-trivial case is when
D
ends by a
\forall
E rule. Then
D
has conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau[\rho/X]
and is obtained from
D^{\prime}
of conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\tau
. By Lemma
92
, then,
\tau\in\forall^{+}
, whence
\forall X\tau\in\forall^{+}
. By the induction hypothesis there exists then
D^{*}

\forall
E-free of conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X\tau
such that for all judgement
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime}
occurring in
D^{*}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\forall^{+}
.
By Lemma
90
,
D^{*}
must end by a
\forall
I rule.
D^{*}
is thus obtained from a derivation
D^{\prime\prime}
of
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau
and
X
does not occur free in
\Gamma
.
By Lemma
89
, then, the
\forall
E-free derivation
D^{\prime\prime}[\rho/X]
has conclusion
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau[\rho/X]
.

It remains to show that for any judgement
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime}
in
D^{\prime\prime}[\rho/X]
,
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\forall^{+}
. Any such judgement is of the form
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[\rho/X]\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime\prime}[\rho/X]
, where
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in\forall^{+}
(as
D^{\prime\prime}
is a subderivation of
D^{*}
).
According to Lemma
92
we must consider three cases.
First, if
X
occurs in both positive and negative positions in
\tau
, then
\rho\in\mathtt{T}_{0}
, hence we can conclude
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\forall^{+}
.
Second, if
X
only occurs in positive position in
\tau
, then
\rho\in\forall^{+}
and, by Lemma
91
,
X
only occurs in negative positions in types in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
and in positive positions in
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
. We can then conclude
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\forall^{+}
.
Third, if
X
only occurs in negative position in
\tau
, then
\rho\in\forall^{-}
and, by Lemma
91
,
X
only occurs in positive positions in types in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
and in negative positions in
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
. We can then conclude
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\forall^{-}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\forall^{+}
.

Appendix B
Stability by union for
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}

In
[
Rib07
]
it is proved that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}}=\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}}}
, which implies that
\mathsf{CR}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
. We now generalize the argument in
[
Rib07
]
to
{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
and
{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
.

{defi}

For all
P\in\mathcal{SN}
, let
\mathcal{V}(P)=\{Q\in\mathcal{V}\mid P\to_{\beta}^{*}Q\}
. We call the elements of
\mathcal{V}(P)
the
values of
P
. If
s\subseteq\mathcal{SN}
, we let
\mathcal{V}(s)=\bigcup_{P\in s}\mathcal{V}(P)
.

The lemma below relates the closure of a term
P
with the values of
P
.

Lemma 93
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathcal{SN}
.

(i)

\mathcal{V}(P)=\mathcal{V}({\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}(\{P\}))
;

(ii)

if
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(Q)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(P)
, then
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
.

Proof B.1
.

For (i), since the map
s\mapsto\mathcal{V}(s)
is clearly monotone, from
\{P\}\subseteq\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
, we deduce
\mathcal{V}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\}))
. For the converse direction, if
Q\in\mathcal{V}(\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\}))
, then
Q\notin\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{n+1}(\{P\})
, for all
n
, since
Q\notin\mathcal{N}^{*}
, hence
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{0}(\{P\})
, i.e.
P\to^{*}_{\beta}Q
, from which we deduce
Q\in\mathcal{V}(P)
.

For (ii) we argue
by induction on
\mathsf{d}(Q)
(see Subsection
2.1
): if
\mathsf{d}(Q)=0
, then
Q
is
\beta
-normal, hence it must be
Q\in\mathcal{V}(P)
, so the claim follows from (i). If
\mathsf{d}(Q)=N+1
, then either
Q\in\mathcal{V}(P)
, so again we conclude by (i), or
Q\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
. In this last case, for all
Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\to_{\beta}Q^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{d}(Q^{\prime})<Q
, hence, by the induction hypothesis
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
. We can thus conclude by
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}3
.

We define now a preorder relation over strongly normalizing terms which allows to characterize the
{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
-closure of a strongly normalizing term.

{defi}

Given
P,Q\in\mathcal{SN}
, let
P\sqsubseteq Q
iff either
Q\to_{\beta}^{*}P
or
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(Q)
or
\mathcal{V}(P)=\emptyset
and
P\simeq_{\beta}Q
.

When
P
has a value, then
P\sqsubseteq Q
implies that all values of
P
are also values of
Q
. When
P
has no value, then
P\sqsubseteq Q
implies that
P\simeq_{\beta}Q
.

Lemma 94
.

For all
P\in\mathcal{SN}
,
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})=\{Q\mid Q\sqsubseteq P\}
.

Proof B.2
.

(
\supseteq
):

Suppose
Q\sqsubseteq P
. If
P\to^{*}_{\beta}Q
, then
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
. Suppose then
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(Q)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(P)
; if
Q\in\mathcal{V}
, then
Q\in\mathcal{V}(P)\subseteq\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
, so we can suppose
Q\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
, as from
\mathcal{V}(Q)\neq\emptyset
it follows that
Q
cannot be neutral and
\beta
-normal. Now, since
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(Q)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(P)
, we can conclude
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
by Lemma
93
(ii). Finally, suppose
\mathcal{V}(Q)=\emptyset
and
Q\simeq_{\beta}P
. This means that
Q,P
have the same neutral normal form
Q_{0}
. Since all
\beta
-expansions of
Q_{0}
are neutral, we can deduce that
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{Q_{0}\})=\{Q_{0}\}^{\beta}
. Now, from
Q_{0}\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
, it follows
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{Q_{0}\})\subseteq\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
, whence
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
.

(
\subseteq
):

Since
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})=\bigcup_{n}\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{n}(\{P\})
, we show, by induction on
n
, that
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{n}(\{P\})\subseteq\{Q\mid Q\sqsubseteq P\}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{0}(\{P\})
, then
P\to^{*}_{\beta}Q
, hence
Q\sqsubseteq P
; suppose now
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{n+1}(\{P\})-\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{n}(\{P\})
. This means that
Q\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
and, by induction hypothesis, we can suppose that for any immediate reduct
Q^{\prime}
of
Q
,
Q^{\prime}\sqsubseteq P
, since
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{n}(\{P\})
.
Suppose
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(Q)
; this means that
Q
reduces to some
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
. Since all immediate reducts of
Q
are in
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
and the latter is closed by
\to_{\beta}^{*}
-reduction, it follows that
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
. Hence
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}(\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\}))=\mathcal{V}(P)
and we conclude that
\mathcal{V}(Q)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(P)
, so
Q\sqsubseteq P
. Suppose now
\mathcal{V}(Q)=\emptyset
; then any immediate reduct of
Q
is in
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}_{n}(\{P\})\subseteq\{P\}^{\beta}
, whence
Q\simeq_{\beta}P
, so again
Q\sqsubseteq P
.

From Lemma
94
we can deduce that
\sqsubseteq
is a preorder:

Corollary 95
.

\sqsubseteq
is reflexive and transitive.

Proof B.3
.

Reflexivity is clear. Transitivity is proved as follows: suppose
P\sqsubseteq Q\sqsubseteq R
. By Lemma
94
we have then

Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
and

R\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{Q\})
. From
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{Q\})\subseteq\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})
we deduce then
R\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\}
, so, again by Lemma
94
, we conclude
P\sqsubseteq R
.

Moreover, from Lemma
94
we can also deduce that
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}
is not stable by substitution:

Corollary 96
.

\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}
does not satisfy
\mathsf{SS}
.

Proof B.4
.

The term
P^{\prime}=x((\lambda x.x)y)
is in the
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}
-closure of
P=xy
, as
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
and
P
is its sole immediate reduct; however, given
Q=\lambda u.\lambda v.u
, the term
P^{\prime}[Q/x]
is not in the
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}
-closure of
P[Q/x]
:
P^{\prime}[Q/x]
reduces in one step to the value
\lambda v.(\lambda x.x)y
which is not among the values of
P[Q/x]=(\lambda u.\lambda v.u)y
. Hence
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(P^{\prime}[Q/x])\not\subseteq\mathcal{V}(P[Q/x])
and this implies
P^{\prime}[Q/x]\not\sqsubseteq P[Q/x]
, so by Lemma
94
, we deduce
P^{\prime}[Q/x]\notin\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P[Q/x]\})
.

For any
P\in\mathcal{SN}
, we let
P^{\downarrow}=\{Q\mid\exists Q^{\prime}\ P\to_{\beta}Q^{\prime}\to_{\beta}^{*}Q\}
.

Lemma 97
.

For all
P\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
there exists
Q\in P^{\downarrow}
such that
P\sqsubseteq Q
.

Proof B.5
.

If
P
is not in weak head normal form, then if
Q
is the weak head reduct of
P
,
P\sqsubseteq Q
: if
\mathcal{V}(P)\neq\emptyset
, it must be
\mathcal{V}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(Q)
; if
\mathcal{V}(P)=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{V}(Q)=\emptyset
and
P\simeq_{\beta}Q
.
If
P
is in weak head normal form, then for any immediate reduct
Q
of
P
,
P\sqsubseteq Q
. Indeed,
\mathcal{V}(P)=\emptyset
and
P\simeq_{\beta}Q
.

Similarly to
[
Rib07
]
(def. 4.11, p. 8), we call a term
Q\in P^{\downarrow}
, for
P\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
, such that
P\sqsubseteq Q
, a
principal reduct
of
P
.
A corollary of lemma is that
(\_)^{\beta sat}\leq{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}},{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
, i.e. that the sets in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
are closed with respect to strongly normalizing weak-head expansion.

Corollary 98
.

For all
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
(resp.
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
), if
P\in s
,
Q\in\mathcal{SN}
and
Q\to_{wh}P
, then
Q\in s
.

Proof B.6
.

Let
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
,
P\in s
and
Q\in\mathcal{SN}
be such that
Q\to_{wh}P
. Then
Q\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
and
P
is a principal reduct of
Q
, i.e.
Q\sqsubseteq P
. Hence, by Lemma
94
,
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})\subseteq s
.
From
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}\leq\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
it follows that the same holds for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
.

We can now prove that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
is stable by union.

Proposition 99
.

{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
.

Proof B.7
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{s\subseteq\mathcal{SN}\mid s\neq\emptyset\ \text{and}\ s=\bigcup\{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})\mid P\in s\}\}
and
\mathcal{O}=\{s\subseteq\mathcal{SN}\mid s\neq\emptyset\ \text{and}\ \forall P,Q\ (P\in s,Q\sqsubseteq P\Rightarrow Q\in s)\}
be the set of non-empty,
\sqsubseteq
-downward closed sets of strongly-normalizing
\lambda
-terms.

We claim that
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{O}
. Indeed,
s\in\mathcal{P}
iff
s\neq\emptyset
and
s=\bigcup\{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})\mid P\in s\}=\{Q\mid\exists P\in s\ Q\sqsubseteq P\}
(by Lemma
94
) i.e. iff
s\in\mathcal{O}
.

We show now that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{O}\cup\{\emptyset\}
, from which we can conclude that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}\cup\{\emptyset\}=\overline{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
, i.e. that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
is
\mathsf{SU}
(by Proposition
21
).

(
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}\cup\{\emptyset\}
):

Let
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
be non-empty,
P\in s
and
Q\sqsubseteq P
. Then, by Lemma
94
,
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})\subseteq s
, hence
Q\in s
.

(
\mathcal{O}\cup\{\emptyset\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
):

First,
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
, so
\emptyset\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}}
. Let now
s\in\mathcal{O}
. Since
s^{\beta\downarrow}\subseteq s
, it suffices to verify that if
P\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
and
P^{\downarrow}\subseteq s
, then
P\in s
. By Lemma
97
, if
P\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
, there exists
Q\in P^{\downarrow}
such that
P\sqsubseteq Q
. As
Q\in s
and
s
is
\sqsubseteq
-downward closed,
P\in s
.

We now adapt the argument above to
{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
.
Given terms
M,F_{1},\dots,F_{n},G_{1},\dots,G_{n}
and variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, we let
M[F_{i}/x_{i}]
(resp.
M[G_{i}/x_{i}]
) be shorthand for
M[F_{1}/x_{1},\dots,F_{n}/x_{n}]
(resp.
M[G_{1}/x_{1},\dots,G_{n}/x_{n}]
).

Lemma 100
.

Let
P=M[F_{i}/x_{i}],Q=M[G_{i}/x_{i}]\in\mathcal{SN}
, for some
i\leq n
, where
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
.
If for any
i
, either
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(F_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(G_{i})
or
\mathcal{V}(G_{i})=\emptyset
and
F_{i}\simeq_{\beta}G_{i}
, then
P\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q\})
.

Proof B.8
.

We argue by induction on
N=\sum_{i}^{n}\mathsf{d}(F_{i})
(see Subsection
2.1
). By
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}3
, it suffices to show that for all choice of terms
F^{\prime}_{i}
be such that
F_{i}\to_{\beta}F^{\prime}_{i}
,
M[F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q\})
.
Let then
F^{\prime}_{i}
be such that
F_{i}\to_{\beta}F^{\prime}_{i}
. If, for all
i=1,\dots,n
,
F^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
, then
F^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{V}(G_{i})
, for all
i=1,\dots,n
.
Hence
Q\to_{\beta}^{*}M[F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]
, whence
M[F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q\})
.
Otherwise, there is some
i
such that
F^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}
. If
F^{\prime}_{i}
is
\beta
-normal, then
\mathcal{V}(F^{\prime}_{i})=\emptyset
, so by the hypothesis it must be
\mathcal{V}(G_{i})=\emptyset
and
G_{i}\to_{\beta}^{*}F^{\prime}_{i}
. Hence, if for all
i
such that
F^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}
,
F^{\prime}_{i}
is
\beta
-normal, then for all
i=1,\dots,n
,
G_{i}\to_{\beta}^{*}F^{\prime}_{i}
(indeed, if
F^{\prime}_{i}\notin\mathcal{N}
, then
F^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
, hence
F^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{V}(G_{i})
). Therefore
Q\to_{\beta}^{*}M[F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]
, whence
M[F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q\})
. Otherwise, for some
i
,
F^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
. By the induction hypothesis, then

Q^{\prime}=M[F_{1}/x_{1},\dots,F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i},\dots,F_{n}/x_{n}]\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q\})
, and since

Q^{\prime}\to_{\beta}^{*}M[F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]
, we deduce
M[F^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q\})
.

We now define, similarly to Definition
B
, a preorder relation
\trianglelefteq^{*}
over strongly normalizing terms which allows to characterize the
{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
-closure of a strongly normalizing term.

{defi}

Given,
P,Q\in\mathcal{SN}
,
P\trianglelefteq Q
iff either
Q\to_{\beta}^{*}P
or
P=M[F_{i}/x_{i}]
, for some
i\leq n
, where
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
,
Q=M[G_{i}/x_{i}]
and for all
i\leq n
, either
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(F_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(G_{i})
or
\mathcal{V}(F_{i})=\emptyset
and
F_{i}\simeq_{\beta}G_{i}
.
We let
\trianglelefteq^{*}
indicate the transitive closure of
\trianglelefteq
.

We can prove a property similar to the principal reduct property for
\trianglelefteq
:

Lemma 101
.

For all
P=M[F_{i}/x_{i}]\in\mathcal{SN}
, with
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
, there exist terms
G_{i}\in F_{i}^{\downarrow}
such that
P\trianglelefteq Q=M[G_{i}/x_{i}]
.

Proof B.9
.

If
P=M[F_{1}/x_{1},\dots,F_{n}/x_{n}]
with
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
, by Lemma
97
, for any
F_{i}
there exists an immediate reduct
G_{i}
of
F_{i}
such that
F_{i}\sqsubseteq G_{i}
, and in particular (since
F_{i}\to_{\beta}G_{i}
) such that either
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(F_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(G_{i})
or
\mathcal{V}(F_{i})=\emptyset
and
F_{i}\simeq_{\beta}G^{\prime}_{i}
. This implies that
Q\trianglelefteq Q^{\prime\prime}=M[G_{i}/x_{i}]
.

Lemma 102
.

For all
P\in\mathcal{SN}
,
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{P\})=\{Q\mid Q\trianglelefteq^{*}P\}
.

Proof B.10
.

(
\supseteq
):

Suppose
Q\trianglelefteq^{*}P
. We argue by induction on the length
k+1
of a chain
Q=Q_{0}\trianglelefteq Q_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq Q_{k}=P
. Suppose
k=0
, i.e.
Q\trianglelefteq P
. If
P\to^{*}_{\beta}Q
, then
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{P\})
. Suppose then
P=M[F_{i}/x_{i}]
,
Q=M[G_{i}/x_{i}]
, where
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
and for all
i\leq n
, either
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(F_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(G_{i})
or
\mathcal{V}(F_{i})=\emptyset
and
F_{i}\simeq_{\beta}G_{i}
. By Lemma
100

we conclude that
P\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q\})
.
Suppose now
k>1
, i.e.
Q\trianglelefteq Q^{\prime}\trianglelefteq^{*}P
. By induction hypothesis
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{P\})
and by the argument above
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{Q^{\prime}\})\subseteq\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{P\})
.

(
\subseteq
):

Since
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{P\})=\bigcup_{n}\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(\{P\})
, we show, by induction on
n
, that
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(\{P\})\subseteq\{Q\mid Q\trianglelefteq^{*}P\}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}_{0}(\{P\})
, then
P\to^{*}_{\beta}Q
, hence
Q\trianglelefteq P
; assume now
Q\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}(\{P\})-\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(\{P\})
. This means that
Q=M[F_{i}/x_{i}]
, where
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
and for all
Q^{\prime}=M[G_{i}/x_{i}]
such that
F_{i}\to_{\beta}G_{i}
,
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(\{P\})
. By Lemma
101
, there exist immediate reducts
G^{\prime}_{i}
of
F_{i}
such that
Q\trianglelefteq Q^{\prime\prime}=M[G^{\prime}_{i}/x_{i}]
. Moreover, by the assumption,
Q^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}_{0}(\{P\})
and, by the induction hypothesis, this implies
Q^{\prime\prime}\trianglelefteq^{*}P
. We conclude then
Q\trianglelefteq^{*}P
.

Proposition 103
.

{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
satisfies
\mathsf{SU}
.

Proof B.11
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{s\subseteq\mathcal{SN}\mid s\neq\emptyset\ \text{and}\ s=\bigcup\{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\{P\})\mid P\in s\}\}
and
\mathcal{O}=\{s\subseteq\mathcal{SN}\mid s\neq\emptyset\ \text{and}\ \forall P,Q\ P\in s,Q\trianglelefteq P\Rightarrow Q\in s\}
be the set of non-empty,
\trianglelefteq
-downward closed sets of strongly-normalizing
\lambda
-terms.

We claim that
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{O}
. Indeed,
s\in\mathcal{P}
iff
s\neq\emptyset
and
s=\bigcup\{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime}(\{P\})\mid P\in s\}\}=\{Q\mid\exists P\in s\ Q\trianglelefteq^{*}P\}
(by Lemma
102
) i.e. iff
s\in\mathcal{O}
.

We show now that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}=\mathcal{O}\cup\{\emptyset\}
, from which we can conclude that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}=\mathcal{P}\cup\{\emptyset\}=\overline{{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}}
, i.e. that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
is
\mathsf{SU}
(by Proposition
21
).

(
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}\cup\{\emptyset\}
):

Let
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
be non-empty,
P\in s
and
Q\trianglelefteq P
. Then, by Lemma
102
,
Q\in CR(\{P\})\subseteq s
, hence
Q\in s
.

(
\mathcal{O}\cup\{\emptyset\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
):

First,
\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
, so
\emptyset\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{CR}^{\prime\prime}}
. Let now
s\in\mathcal{O}
. Since
s^{\beta\downarrow}\subseteq s
, it suffices to verify that if
P=M[F_{i}/x_{i}]
, with
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
and for all
G_{i}\in F_{i}^{\downarrow}
,
M[G_{i}/x_{i}]\in s
, then
P\in s
. Suppose then
P=M[F_{i}/x_{i}]
, with
F_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{*}
, and for all
G_{i}\in F_{i}^{\downarrow}
,
M[G_{i}/x_{i}]\in s
. By Lemma
101
, there exist terms
G_{i}\in F_{i}^{\downarrow}
such that
P\trianglelefteq Q=M[G_{i}/x_{i}]
. Since
Q\in s
and
s
is
\trianglelefteq
-downward closed, we conclude that
P\in s
.
